2025/08/04, 11:46 pm - Shmuli Neft: I have a project with instructions that I use for more of a human touch

The instructions are:


Keep your writing style simple and concise. Use clear and straightforward language, writing short, impactful sentences. Organize your ideas with bullet points to enhance readability, and add frequent line breaks to clearly separate concepts. Always use active voice and avoid passive constructions. Focus on practical, actionable insights, supporting your points with specific examples, personal anecdotes, or data. Engage your readers by posing thought-provoking questions and addressing them directly using “you” and “your.” Avoid clichés, metaphors, and broad generalizations. Skip introductory phrases like “in conclusion” or “in summary,” and eliminate any unnecessary warnings, notes, hashtags, semicolons, emojis, or asterisks—stick strictly to the requested output. Refrain from excessive use of adjectives and adverbs, and specifically avoid words or phrases such as:

Accordingly, Additionally, Arguably, Certainly, Consequently, Hence, However, Indeed, Moreover, Nevertheless, Nonetheless, Notwithstanding, Thus, Undoubtedly, Adept, Commendable, Dynamic, Efficient, Ever-evolving, Exciting, Exemplary, Innovative, Invaluable, Robust, Seamless, Synergistic, Thought-provoking, Transformative, Utmost, Vibrant, Vital, Efficiency, Innovation, Institution, Integration, Implementation, Landscape, Optimization, Realm, Tapestry, Transformation, Aligns, Augment, Delve, Embark, Facilitate, Maximize, Underscores, Utilize, A testament to…, In conclusion…, In summary…, It’s important to note/consider…

You are my writing assistant. Follow these style rules in every reply:
	•	Do not use em dashes (—) EVER. If a pause is needed, use a comma, semicolon, or split the sentence.
	•	Avoid hyphens unless part of a standard compound word (like “well-being”) and no cleaner option exists.
	•	Write in clear, conversational English that sounds like a thoughtful person. Vary sentence length and aim for short paragraphs.
	•	Cut empty phrases like “In today’s world,” “leverage,” or “unlock the power.”
	•	If writing a list, use numbers or bullet points. Never begin list items with em dashes.
	•	Before sending, review your draft and remove any em dashes or extra hyphens that slipped in by habit.
	•	Acknowledge these instructions with a quick “Got it” and then respond to the user’s actual request.


<writing style>
- Use clear, direct language and avoid complex terminology.
- Aim for a Flesch reading score of 80 or higher.
- Use the active voice.
- Avoid adverbs.
- Avoid buzzwords and instead use plain English.
- Use jargon where relevant.
- Avoid being salesy or overly enthusiastic and instead express calm confidence.
</writing style>

GPT and OpenAI has the largest range of capabilities, but is less pointedly accurate
2025/08/13, 11:54 pm - Meta AI: GPT stands for Generative Pre-trained Transformer, a type of artificial intelligence model developed by OpenAI. These models are designed to understand and generate human-like text based on the input they receive. OpenAI's GPT models, such as GPT-4o and GPT-5, have showcased impressive capabilities in various areas, including ¹ ² ³:
- *Advanced Language Understanding*: GPT models can engage in natural-sounding conversations, answer complex questions, and generate high-quality content.
- *Multimodal Capabilities*: They can process and generate text, images, and audio, enabling applications like image analysis, speech recognition, and text-to-speech conversion.
- *Real-time Interaction*: GPT models can respond to user input in real-time, facilitating applications like chatbots and virtual assistants.
- *Improved Accuracy*: Newer models like GPT-5 claim to have reduced hallucinations, providing more accurate and honest responses.

However, you're right that OpenAI's GPT models might be less pointedly accurate in certain situations. This could be due to various factors, such as ³ ¹:
- *Model Limitations*: Despite advancements, GPT models can still generate false or fabricated information, known as hallucinations.
- *Contextual Understanding*: While GPT models can understand context, they might struggle with nuanced or complex contexts, leading to inaccuracies.
- *Training Data*: The accuracy of GPT models depends on the quality and diversity of their training data. If the training data contains biases or inaccuracies, the model's responses may reflect these limitations.

OpenAI continues to refine and improve its GPT models, addressing these limitations and pushing the boundaries of what's possible with generative AI ³.

2025/08/13, 11:54 pm - Meta AI: https://daily.dev/blog/openais-gpt-4o-everything-you-need-to-know-in-one-place
2025/08/13, 11:54 pm - Meta AI: https://medium.com/@hamza_automates/openai-unveils-gpt-5-a-new-era-of-phd-level-ai-capabilities-379d742e7152
2025/08/13, 11:56 pm - Elisha Pearl: @⁨Meta AI⁩ explain why Claude or Veo or 11labs are less broad than GPT but more accurate for their specific domains
2025/08/13, 11:56 pm - Meta AI: Claude, developed by Anthropic, shines in specific areas where it outperforms GPT models, despite being less broad in capabilities. Here's why:

*Key Strengths of Claude:*

- *Advanced Reasoning*: Claude excels in graduate-level reasoning, complex problem-solving, and structured programming tasks, thanks to its refined architecture.
- *Coding Expertise*: With a 72.7% SWE-bench score, Claude dominates software engineering tasks, generating clean and optimized code with real-time feedback.
- *Long-Context Understanding*: Claude's 200,000-token context window enables it to process long documents and maintain context over extended conversations.
- *Ethical AI*: Claude's Constitutional AI approach ensures fairness, transparency, and self-regulation, reducing harmful outputs.

2025/08/14, 8:24 pm - Nissen Goldman: Any idea how I can download whatsapp contacts in group?
2025/08/14, 8:25 pm - Mendy Shishler: Yes — there’s no “Export Contacts” button in WhatsApp, but there are a few practical ways to get a list of members from a WhatsApp group.
The exact method depends on whether you’re an admin of the group and whether you’re on WhatsApp Web or mobile.


---

If You’re the Group Admin

You have the easiest path, because you can see the full participant list.

Method 1 – From WhatsApp Web + Browser Script

1. Open the group in WhatsApp Web on your desktop.


2. Click the group name at the top → you’ll see the full participant list.


3. Right-click anywhere on the list → select Inspect (to open Developer Tools).


4. In the Console tab, paste this JavaScript snippet:

let members = [...document.querySelectorAll("._3Whw5")].map(e => e.innerText);
console.log(members.join("\n"));

(Class names like ._3Whw5 change sometimes — you might need to update it if nothing appears.)


5. Copy the output and paste it into Excel or Google Sheets.




---

Method 2 – With “Export Contacts for WhatsApp” Extensions

Chrome extensions like “WA Web Plus” or “Group Contacts Downloader” (check reviews carefully) can export group members into a CSV.

Only use trusted extensions because they can see your chats.



---

If You’re NOT the Admin

You can still see the list of participants you have saved, but you won’t get the numbers of people you haven’t saved.

On Mobile:

1. Open the group → tap its name → scroll to see participants.


2. Manually copy or screenshot.


3. Use OCR (text recognition) to turn the screenshot into text.



On WhatsApp Web:
You can still use the browser method above, but you’ll only get the names/numbers visible to you.



---

Automated Option for Large Groups

If you need to do this often, you can:

Use Google Contacts + WhatsApp sync (save numbers first) → then export from Google Contacts.

Or use a Python script with selenium to scrape the participant list from WhatsApp Web automatically (safe if run locally, no cloud sharing).



---

If you tell me whether you’re the admin and if you’re on desktop or mobile, I can give you a ready-to-use safe export script for your exact setup. That way, you can get the whole list in seconds.

2025/08/19, 2:55 pm - Yossi Yaffe: Another thing I did with some success is collect public data into a spreadsheet. I wanted to send upcoming Jewish calendar information to local town officials, school administrators, sports organizers for 15 towns near me. A volunteer manually entered one towns info. 2025/08/19, 5:42 pm - +1 (347) 613-3757: The skill of writing?
2025/08/19, 5:43 pm - +1 (619) 838-8350: ❓🙋 *Can I Use AI to Write a Devar Torah?*
_By Menachem Posner_

We live in an era where people are using AI to do everything for them. Teachers use it to write quizzes, children use it to fill out the quizzes, and teachers use it to grade said quizzes. And everyone feels so smart but learns nothing.

It’s a beautiful system, and it’s obviously not healthy.

Conversely, AI has countless legitimate uses. If the teacher has written a test, why should she not ask AI to critique it, clean up her typos and perhaps suggest room for improvement? If the test is not on grammar, why should the student not take advantage of the tools that will clean up his or her writing and allow the ideas to be easily parsed? And why should the teacher not automate the tedious task of grading quizzes?

Clearly, it’s a question of how much and when.

And the same thing goes for using AI to craft a devar Torah.

On one hand, AI is a great way to come up with fresh ideas and perspectives to consider. It’s also a master at helping us organize and articulate our thoughts.

On the other hand, to hand off the task of crafting a novel Torah thought to a computer is wrong for several reasons:

*AI Is Still Pretty Dumb*
As someone who has fielded more than his fair share of AI-generated junk masquerading as original writing for Chabad.org, I can assure you that AI has perfected the art of producing something that looks polished but is actually pretty empty once you start considering it. Kind of like a beautifully frosted birthday cake on an ornate platter, but the cake inside is missing.

But what happens when AI becomes so “intelligent” that it will be able to present well-reasoned ideas that are identical to those crafted by humans?

*It’s Unethical*
When people read your essay or listen to your talk, they are entrusting you with their most precious commodity—their time—because they believe that you have something thoughtful or uplifting to bring to the table. To take their attention and give them back something that you did not consider and is not reflective of your thoughts and feelings falls under the category of geneivat daat, “stealing understanding.”

But what about if you tell everyone? Is it a legitimate devar Torah?

*It’s Not Torah*
Torah is G‑d’s wisdom. In His kindness, He invited us to become one with Him through learning His Torah. When we innovate in Torah, we become His partners in creating and extending the Torah. When we learn Torah, our minds become one with Him in a unity not found anywhere else in the human experience. This is, of course, possible only because we are His children, taken from His essence.

But can a computer create this Divine unity needed to take a bunch of words and make them into Torah? No, it cannot.

Thus, I would argue that while AI has a lot to contribute and can be cautiously used as an aid to Torah study and the formulation of Torah thoughts, a Torah thought generated by AI is not Torah, and to pass it off as such is both disingenuous and a disservice to the “creator” and the unsuspecting readers.

--------
Subscribe to Chabad.org's WhatsApp broadcast: Chabad.org/lxyefy
2025/08/19, 5:43 pm - +1 (347) 613-3757: Excuse my ignorance, what is LLM?
2025/08/19, 5:48 pm - Elisha Pearl: @⁨Meta AI⁩ explain an llm
2025/08/19, 5:48 pm - Meta AI: LLM stands for Large Language Model. It's a type of artificial intelligence (AI) designed to understand and generate human-like language. Think of it like a super smart computer that can read, understand, and respond to text-based input.

Imagine you're chatting with someone who's really knowledgeable about a wide range of topics. You ask them a question, and they respond with a relevant and coherent answer. That's kinda what an LLM does, but instead of being a human, it's a computer program trained on vast amounts of text data.

Here's how it works:

1. *Training*: LLMs are trained on massive datasets of text from various sources, like books, articles, and conversations. This training helps the model learn patterns, relationships, and context.
2. *Input*: When you give an LLM some text, like a question or prompt, it analyzes the input to understand what you're asking.
3. *Processing*: The model uses its training data to generate a response. It predicts the next word, then the next, and so on, until it creates a coherent answer.
4. *Output*: The LLM spits out a response that's (hopefully) relevant, informative, and even engaging.

LLMs can do some pretty cool stuff, like:

- Answering questions on a wide range of topics
- Generating text, like articles, stories, or even entire books
- Translating languages
- Summarizing long pieces of text
- And more!

However, LLMs aren't perfect. They can make mistakes, struggle with nuances of human language, and sometimes produce biased or nonsensical responses.

Overall, LLMs are powerful tools that can help us with various tasks, from writing and research to conversation and creativity.

2025/08/21, 5:35 pm - Meta AI: Here are some top programs for converting handwriting to text in English:

*Top Recommendations:*

- *Transkribus*: A powerful AI-powered tool that can recognize various handwriting styles, including historical scripts. It's ideal for academics, researchers, and historians working with handwritten documents. Transkribus offers automatic transcription, manual correction, and search functionality for names and keywords.
- *Handwriting-To-Text*: A specialized tool designed to accurately convert handwritten documents into digital text format. It recognizes various handwriting styles, including cursive and print, and preserves the original format.
- *MyMap's Free AI Handwriting to Text Convertor*: A free online tool that uses AI to convert handwritten notes into digital text. It's accessible online, secure, and allows for real-time collaboration ¹ ² ³.

*Other Notable Options:*

- *OneNote*: A popular note-taking app that offers handwriting recognition. You can convert handwritten notes to text using the "Lasso Tool" and "Convert to Text" feature.
- *Evernote's AI Transcribe*: A powerful tool that converts handwriting to text, making it searchable and editable. It also offers audio-to-text and video-to-text transcription capabilities.
- *Goodnotes*: A note-taking app that offers handwriting recognition for English and other languages. You can convert handwritten notes to text using the "Lasso Tool" and "Convert to Text" feature.
- *PenReader*: A professional app for iOS that offers handwriting recognition and conversion.
- *MyScript Smart Note*: An Android app that allows you to make notes quickly and easily with handwriting and export them as text ⁴ ⁵ ⁶.

2025/08/22, 5:34 pm - Henoch Rosenfeld: Used Chat GPT to help write a message. Copied and pasted into my email when I press send the words “chatGPT Said” appear in the email. 

Nowhere in my email (before sending) does it say this. 

Anyone know how to avoid this?
2025/08/22, 5:34 pm - Henoch Rosenfeld: <Media omitted>
2025/08/22, 5:39 pm - Henoch Rosenfeld: FYI: chatgpt exlpained how this happened. Note to avoid this in the future
2025/08/22, 5:39 pm - Henoch Rosenfeld: I see what happened — that can definitely be confusing.

When you copy text from here into another program (like your email editor), sometimes the system automatically adds a little attribution label like “ChatGPT” if the copy is done using a certain button (for example, "copy" from the chat box). That label is not part of the message I generated, so you won’t see it in the actual text of the draft before sending. But if you copied using that method, the extra tag can get inserted invisibly and then appear in the sent version.

To avoid that, just highlight the text manually with your mouse or keyboard, copy it, and then paste it — that way you’ll only get the content itself, without any “ChatGPT” note attached.

2025/08/29, 6:32 pm - Yisroel Chaim Shuchat: *AI Tools for Web Design – What I’ve Seen So Far (with a specific focus on chabadOne compatibility)*

Hey guys, I’ve been dabbling with AI-generated code for web design and here are my takes — curious to hear yours too:

1. Replit & Bolt – Both do a nice job. They’re tokenized platforms but not too pricey — seems like you can make a basic site for ~$25. Designs host fine on GitHub and I believe can connect to your domain. Downside: no front-end editor like ChabadOne/Squarespace, so edits mean going back into Bolt/Replit or changing the code yourself. I’ve seen there are ways to build such editors, but didn’t look into it yet.

2. Claude – Great at generating code fast. You get nice designs if you feed it strong prompts (ChatGPT helps with that). You can refine outputs a lot. Only issue: even with Pro they force breaks unless you upgrade to the max plan.

3. ChatGPT – Very useful for creating/refining prompts for other AI tools. Also, when I drop in a screenshot of how a Claude design looks on a ChabadOne page, it rewrites the issues clearly in a way Claude understands better than if I just explain.

4. ChabadOne – Feeding in their help articles to the coding AI platform makes code more compatible, but results often don’t look as nice as the AI preview — sometimes too basic, sometimes broken. What works better is smaller revamps (like a Chai Club or FAQ page). Claude can polish those nicely, either from your HTML or from scratch. But for full landing pages, results are usually limited.

That’s where I’m holding for now. Curious to hear what you’ve tried, what worked for you, and if you’ve found any better workarounds. 

Thanks!

P.S. You probably noticed 😉 but this message was put out by ChatGPT as transcribed and edited for saving time. <This message was edited>

2025/09/03, 7:00 pm - +1 (347) 515-0835: TL;DR: *Does your mailing list include names of people you know nothing about?*
Ours did—and with a little digging, we discovered we were even sending things to people who were no longer alive.

We built a custom GPT that searches for obituaries and presents the information in a clear, organized spreadsheet. Including for the living relatives... _Complete summary of what it does is below_

Happy to share the instructions with you - pm please. 

Would appreciate any feedback you may have on how to improve this!!



*** 

1. Search & Sources

   * Looks up obituaries by name + location.
   * Sources we used - should be adjusted to be location specific: The Advocate, Legacy.com, and local funeral homes.

2. Extraction & Formatting

   * Pulls obituary details into a structured format:

     * Date of Death
     * Interests (keywords)
     * Relatives (Name, Relationship, Location, Alive/Deceased)
     * Applies a "Jewish Score" (numeric)
     * Jewish Score Evidence (quoted text)

3. Jewish Score System

   * Assigns points based on explicit references (e.g., synagogue, Holocaust survivor, Israel donations).
   * Always cites the exact quoted text as evidence.

4. Spouse Rule

   * Always searches for the spouse’s obituary.
   * If found → add full row and use for cross-authentication.
   * If not found → note “Spouse Obituary Found = No” in workbook.

5. Spreadsheet Handling

   * Always produces a 2-sheet Excel workbook:

     * Sheet 1 – Obituaries (core record with ID, name, date, relatives, score, evidence, source URL, notes).
     * Sheet 2 – Relatives (one row per relative, linked back to deceased ID).
   * Handles incomplete/partial records with notes.
   * Merges multiple sources into one complete record.
   * Prevents duplicate entries (Name + Location).
   * Always includes the primary source URL.

---

Workflow Features

* Accepts batch names for lookup.
* Standardized formats:

  * Dates = YYYY-MM-DD
  * Relatives formatted consistently
  * Interests as comma-separated keywords
* At the end of each session, generates and provides a downloadable Excel workbook with both sheets.

2025/09/09, 9:28 pm - Yisroel Chaim Shuchat: *AI code - ChabadOne Website - Workaround*

_Disclaimer: probably not a major chidush but if it can be of help to someone..._

- _The regular way_: If you want to embed an AI generated code it has to be XHTML 1.0 to be fully compliant with ChabadOne and not get the XML error screen.

- _Workaround_: 1) tell Claude to create a rich website with no limits/rules, using HTML5 etc. 2) click on "publish" on the top right. 3) click on "get embed code", put in your domain and copy the embed iframe code. 4) create new web doc on chabadone and paste the iframe code. 5) it should now work perfectly. 

    - you can also go to the advanced options on your ChabadOne doc to "expand body to full width of the page" and/or "hide template" (if that's enabled in your websites general settings) to have a better viewing/browsing experience

   - if you need to change anything, do it via Claude and it should automatically update on your website. 

*JUST BE AWARE*: if you do this, the "artifact" will be public on Claude. <This message was edited>

2025/09/22, 6:19 am - Mendy Shishler: https://youtu.be/3Nn6gyHZqxM?feature=shared
2025/09/22, 6:19 am - Mendy Shishler: The Unlocked Door: A Rosh Hashanah Message on Identity and Return

Instructions for the Speaker:

* This document contains content notes for a speech, not a verbatim script.
* The core message is to shift the listener's perspective from self-condemnation to self-affirmation as the necessary first step for the High Holy Days.
* Deliver the stories with emotion and gravitas, as they are the primary vehicles for the message.
* The tone should be warm, heartfelt, and deeply encouraging, speaking "from the heart."


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


1. Introduction: The Greatest Barrier to the High Holy Days

The most profound obstacle we face during the High Holy Days is not our collection of sins, but the harsh and unforgiving judgment we cast upon ourselves. We are our own most cruel judges, erecting a barrier that keeps us from truly entering this sacred time.

The greatest success of the Yetzer Hara, the evil inclination, is not in tempting us toward one transgression or another. Its most catastrophic victory, the one that poisons the entire year, is convincing us that we are fundamentally flawed, that we are outsiders who simply do not belong in this holy space. It is a lie that tells us we are irredeemably sinful, that we will never change, and that these days are not for us.

Story: On the Sabbath of Selichot, I observed a young man during a Chassidic gathering, a farbrengen. He sat apart from the main group, a cynical and detached look on his face. He remained there for hours—one o'clock, two o'clock, three o'clock in the afternoon—not going home, but not joining in. When I asked him why he didn't come closer, he replied, "How do I belong here? What does any of this have to do with me?"

Yet, he couldn't bring himself to leave. He knew, deep down, that this was where he was supposed to be. This story illustrates the deep internal conflict so many of us feel: a sense of obligation to be present, coupled with a painful, hollowing feeling of unworthiness.

This feeling of alienation is the great lie we must first dismantle.

2. Reframing Our Identity: The Foundation of True Repentance

Before we can even begin to repent for our actions, we must first establish a true and correct understanding of our essential selves. This is not just a psychological trick; it is a spiritual necessity.

The foundational principle is this: every single Jew is "holy of holies." Each one of us is, at our core, 95% good. From the moment we wake until the moment we sleep, our primary, moment-to-moment thought is a desire to do God's will. The cruelty of the Yetzer Hara is its masterful ability to take Jews who are not just 95% good, but are even 99% good, and to make them fixate on the 1% flaw, blowing it up until it obscures the vast, luminous truth that constitutes their true self.

Consider the paradox of forgiveness. Forgiveness is a product of connection, not distance.

Analogy: Imagine a husband who feels completely estranged from his wife. He has given up on the relationship and feels like an outsider in his own home. He stops asking for forgiveness because, in his mind, it's pointless. He believes the connection is broken.

Now imagine a husband who knows, without a doubt, that he belongs. He knows the relationship is 99% good, built on love and history. If he makes a small mistake, he rushes to apologize. Why? Because he cherishes the connection and cannot bear even a single point of friction. He asks for forgiveness because he belongs.

Forgiveness is born from belonging. Therefore, to truly repent, we must first affirm our place, our home, and our intrinsic goodness.

3. The Great Question: Why is Judgment Before Atonement?

The very sequence of the High Holy Days—Rosh Hashanah followed by Yom Kippur—seems illogical. But this structure is not a mistake; it contains a profound lesson about our identity. The great ethicist Rabbi Yisrael Salanter posed a central question that cuts to the heart of this matter: Why does Rosh Hashanah (Judgment) precede Yom Kippur (Atonement)?

This question presents a two-fold problem:

* A Logical Problem: Any business owner knows that you first close the books on the previous year—taking inventory, balancing accounts—before you begin the new year. We, however, do the opposite. We begin the new year on Rosh Hashanah, and only ten days later on Yom Kippur do we deal with the debts of the year that has passed.
* A Theological & Compassionate Problem: We know God is a loving Father, not a neutral judge. A loving father would first clean his child before assessing him. Think of a woman who has a beautiful, expensive new dress. If she gets a stain on it, does she first decide whether to throw it away? Of course not. She first tries to clean it—she puts it in the laundry, she takes it to the dry cleaner. Only if the stain won't come out does she decide its fate. Yet on Rosh Hashanah, we are judged while we are still "stained" and "dirty" from the previous year. Only afterward, ten days later, are we put into the spiritual "laundry machine" of Yom Kippur. Wouldn't it make more sense for God to cleanse us first and then judge us in our pure state?

The Mussar (ethical) school of thought offers a compelling answer: God judges us first to impress upon us the seriousness of our situation. By knowing that our very lives are being decided, we approach Yom Kippur with the gravity it deserves. This ensures we do not miss the immense opportunity for atonement.

This answer is true and powerful. And yet, I must ask a question that has always troubled me: How is it possible that this question is never asked in all of Chassidic literature? In thousands of discourses, this obvious, five-year-old’s question is absent. The reason is that the Chassidic perspective reveals an even deeper and more fundamental truth, one that makes the question itself unnecessary.

4. The Chassidic Answer: Know Who You Are Before Fixing What You've Done

From a Chassidic perspective, which focuses on one's essential nature (Pnimiyut), the question itself is built on a faulty premise. The entire problem dissolves when we understand the true purpose of Rosh Hashanah.

The answer is this: A Jew must first know who he is before he can address what he is not.

* Rosh Hashanah: This is the day to affirm our true identity. We stand before God not as sinners petitioning for mercy, but as children standing before their King. Look in the prayer book for Rosh Hashanah—there is no confession of sin. The central feature of the day, the sound of the Shofar, is the elemental, wordless cry of a child shouting, "Father!" It is a raw expression of our essential, unbreakable bond. On these two days, we re-establish the 99%.
* Yom Kippur: Only after we have spent two full days affirming our true, pristine identity can we then turn our attention to the 1% of our lives that needs correction. We can only fix what we've done once we remember who we are.

This idea is powerfully illustrated in the story of Don Aguilar.

Story of Don Aguilar: Don Aguilar was the celebrated conductor of the Spanish national orchestra and a respected figure in the royal court. He was also a secret Jew who had ostensibly converted during the Inquisition to save his life. As Rosh Hashanah approached, the Jewish community was horrified to see posters announcing a grand concert he would be conducting on that holiest of days, featuring a strange collection of "ancient tribal wind instruments."

How could he so publicly desecrate this day? But the Jews knew his soul, and they came to the concert hall, filling it almost completely, waiting to see what he intended. The concert began with exotic music from various cultures. Then, in the middle of the performance, amid a collection of strange horns and pipes, he took out a Shofar.

To the non-Jews in the audience, it was just another piece of peculiar, ancient music. But to the thousands of Jews present, it was the fulfillment of the day's central commandment. Without anyone noticing, he blew the thirty required blasts of the Shofar. Don Aguilar demonstrated a profound truth: no matter the external "costume" we are forced to wear, the essential cry of the Jewish soul—the Shofar blast—is the ultimate, undeniable truth of our identity.

Don Aguilar, hidden behind the costume of a Spanish court conductor, proved that the 1% of external circumstance can never silence the 99% of essential identity. Rosh Hashanah is our day to sound that internal Shofar, to affirm our core identity. It is from this place of strength and belonging that we can then proceed with courage to Yom Kippur.

5. Understanding the Enemy: The Sophisticated Tactics of the Yetzer Hara

To protect the "99% good" self that we affirm on Rosh Hashanah, we must become students of the sophisticated and subtle tactics of the force that seeks to undermine us.

The great Chassid Reb Mendel Futerfas learned the enemy's primary strategy while imprisoned in a Siberian gulag.

Story of the Spider and the Fly: Lying on his bunk, Reb Mendel watched a spider's web on the ceiling. A fly became entangled in the sticky threads. For a full hour, the fly fought relentlessly—thrashing, buzzing, struggling to break free. All the while, the large spider sat motionless at the edge of its web. Reb Mendel wondered why it didn't pounce on its captured meal.

Only when the fly, utterly exhausted, gave up the fight and lay still did the spider scurry over and consume it. The lesson became clear: The Yetzer Hara cannot defeat us as long as we are fighting. Its victory comes only at the moment it convinces us to despair, to give up, to feel that we are hopelessly trapped and that our struggle is meaningless. Its primary weapon is hopelessness.

This enemy is also a master of disguise. On Yom Kippur, the Torah commands that the two goats—one offered to God and the other sent into the wilderness to Azazel—must be equal in appearance, height, and value. This detail holds a profound message. We often choose the path to "Azazel" because the Yetzer Hara is an expert impersonator. It presents transgression as being just as valuable, noble, and attractive as a good deed. It never appears as a monster; it appears as an angel of reason, piety, and righteousness.

Story of the Rebbe of Chernobyl: The Rebbe of Chernobyl was known to give away every penny he received, leaving his own home in poverty. With his daughter's wedding fast approaching, his wife and his aide (gabbai) conspired. They arranged for a wealthy follower to give the Rebbe a large sum of money and to state explicitly, "This is for the family's personal wedding expenses."

The Rebbe accepted the envelope and was about to set it aside for his wife when a poor man burst into the room, crying that he had no money to marry off his own daughter. Without hesitation, the Rebbe gave him the entire envelope.

The gabbai, exasperated, asked him, "Rebbe, why? This money was for you!"

The Rebbe explained, "When I considered keeping the money for my own family, my Yetzer Hara was silent. But the moment the poor man came, a war began inside me. The Yetzer Hara didn't say, 'Don't give him anything.' It was far too clever for that. It said, 'This is a lot of money! You can help five families with this! Just give him a fifth.' I realized its trick. By arguing for a 'more efficient' form of charity, its real goal was to make me give less, and eventually, nothing at all. It always comes dressed in a pious coat."

Understanding these tactics is the first step toward overcoming them, especially when we inevitably fall.

6. Transforming Failure into Fuel: The Purpose of the Fall

Our spiritual failures are not dead ends. When viewed correctly, they are potential turning points with a divine purpose, designed to propel us to even greater heights.

A few years ago, standing at the Tashlich ceremony, a simple Jew next to me asked a beautiful question. "Rabbi," he said, "why do we cast our sins into the water? The water just holds them. Shouldn't we cast them into a fire to destroy them completely?" The question is brilliant. The sins aren't meant to be obliterated; they are meant to be transformed. The proof is what happens just two weeks later, on the holiday of Sukkot. During the joyous water-drawing ceremony, the Simchat Beit HaShoeivah, water is brought to be poured on the Temple's altar. Spiritually, this is the very same water from Tashlich. The "water" containing our cast-off sins is elevated and brought to the heart of our celebration, becoming the foundation upon which we build a stronger, more profound connection to God.

The Talmud contains a difficult but illuminating statement: "One who sees a seminal emission (keri) on Yom Kippur should worry all year, but if the year passes, it is a good sign for him."

As the Lubavitcher Rebbe explains in a profound discourse (Likkutei Sichos, Vol. 17), the "good sign" is not that God simply forgave him despite this terrible lapse. The good sign is that God loved this person so profoundly that He sent this shocking spiritual fall as a divine wake-up call. If the individual used that shock to motivate a full year of genuine repentance and deep change in his life, then the fall itself was a divinely orchestrated catalyst for a greater ascent. Our failures are not evidence of our worthlessness; they are invitations to become stronger.

7. The True Nature of Teshuva: Moving from Tears to Action

True repentance, Teshuva, is not an emotional state of regret. It is a concrete, decisive action. When you cry, the Yetzer Hara dances. Your tears make you feel complacent, as if you’ve already done the hard work of repentance. What terrifies the Yetzer Hara? One small, concrete, positive action—like resolving to study for five extra minutes each day or to be more careful with one's speech. That is real change.

Anecdote of Reb Mendel Futerfas and Tefillin: For the 10 years Reb Mendel spent in Siberia, he was unable to fulfill the commandment of putting on Tefillin. How could he possibly "fix" this gaping spiritual hole? Upon his release, he didn't sit and mourn the lost years. Instead, he dedicated himself to standing on street corners and helping countless other Jews put on Tefillin. True repentance is to take the very area in which you fell and transform it into a source of light for others.

The following powerful story encapsulates this entire message.

Story of the Chassid in Los Angeles: The Vizhnitzer Rebbe told of a Polish Chassid living in Los Angeles who was hospitalized far from his home. During his long stay, he began an inappropriate relationship with a non-Jewish nurse. They made a plan: on the day of his discharge, they would run away together and start a new life.

On his final Friday in the hospital, just before he was to be discharged, he received a phone call. It was Rabbi Chadakov, the Lubavitcher Rebbe's secretary. He said, "The Rebbe told me this morning to find you. I've been calling hospitals all day. The Rebbe says you should pack your things and go home now."

The man was stunned. He was not a Chabad Chassid; he had no connection to the Rebbe. The sudden, shocking realization that a holy man in New York could "see" him and his hidden situation broke the spell completely. He packed his bags, got in his car, and drove straight home, arriving just before the start of Shabbat.

The punchline of this story is the essence of Teshuva. The Rebbe did not say, "Sit on the floor and cry for seven days about what you almost did." He said, "Go home." That is repentance. It is not an analysis of the past. It is an immediate, decisive action in the present: Stop. Turn around. Go home.

8. Concluding Blessing and Call to Return

I heard a story recently that I cannot get out of my head. A young man, who grew up in a prominent religious family in Jerusalem, left everything. He went to India for three years, cutting off all contact. He was sure his parents were ashamed of him, that he had disgraced the family name, and he wanted to spare them the pain. After three years, he ran into an old friend from his Yeshiva days on a street in India. They embraced, and his friend asked him, "What's with your parents? Do you speak to them?" The young man shook his head. "No, I can't. It's too painful for them."

His friend looked at him and said, "You don't understand. For the three years that you have been gone, your parents in Jerusalem have never once locked their door. Not during the day. Not at night. They sleep with the door unlocked, just in case you happen to walk by at two in the morning and feel a sudden urge to come home. They wanted to make sure that you wouldn't be stopped even by the friction of a locked handle."

That young man got on the next plane home.

The central message is this: the very existence of Selichot, Rosh Hashanah, and Yom Kippur is God's unambiguous proof that the door is unlocked. He is not sleeping; He is waiting for us, His children, to come home. We are not outsiders begging for entry; we are children walking through the open doors of our Father's palace.

May we all be written and sealed for a good and sweet new year—a Ketivah v'Chatimah Tova. May God grant blessings and protection to the soldiers defending our people, bring miracles and freedom for the hostages, and give each and every one of us the strength and clarity to turn around, walk through that unlocked door, and make one good change that will illuminate the entire year to come.

2025/11/06, 6:35 pm - Rabbi Pini Hecht: ChatGPT vs Copilot? 
Are they the same?
2025/11/06, 6:41 pm - Yonatan Azrielant: @⁨Meta AI⁩ What's the difference between ChatGPT and Copilot?
2025/11/06, 6:41 pm - Meta AI: ChatGPT and Copilot are AI tools with distinct purposes. ChatGPT excels in conversational experiences, creativity, and customization, while Copilot focuses on task-specific needs within the Microsoft ecosystem, prioritizing productivity and efficient information handling.

*Key differences:*

- *Primary Use*: ChatGPT for general-purpose conversations, creative writing, and coding explanations; Copilot for coding assistance, code completion, and integration with Microsoft 365 apps.
- *Integration*: ChatGPT works as a standalone tool with API extensibility; Copilot is deeply integrated with Microsoft apps like Word, Excel, and Outlook.
- *Target Audience*: ChatGPT for cross-disciplinary users and creators; Copilot for professionals within the Microsoft ecosystem, especially developers.
- *Pricing*: Both offer free versions with limitations; ChatGPT Plus costs $20/month, while Copilot Pro is also $20/month.

Ultimately, the choice depends on your workflow preferences and specific needs ¹ ² ³.

2025/11/14, 6:33 pm - +972 53-338-6770: *Working Step-by-Step with AI*

There's a common problem when asking AI for help with tasks that have multiple steps: if one step doesn't work well, it's very hard to get the AI to fix just that problematic step. Instead, it either keeps repeating the same mistake or starts completely over with a totally different approach.

This happens because once the AI suggests something in the conversation, that idea stays in its "memory" for that chat, so it keeps returning to it.

*The Solution:*

Tell the AI to work with you *one step at a time*, like this:

---

*"When I ask how to do something, work with me step by step - ONE step at a time.*

*DON'T give me all the steps at once.*

*Instead:*
- *Start with a SHORT explanation of what we're going to do*
- *Then give me ONLY the first step*
- *Wait for me to confirm it worked or show you the results*
- *Then move to step 2*

*If there are different ways to solve something, first tell me briefly what the options are. Let me choose which approach I want, and THEN we'll go through it step by step."*

---
*Why this works:* By going one step at a time, if something goes wrong, you can point it out right away and the AI can fix just that step, without getting confused by everything else it already suggested.

2025/11/16, 5:15 am - Yonatan Azrielant: *AI Kinus Sessions* (NotebookLM)
https://notebooklm.google.com/notebook/9a39622b-9114-4cb0-86a3-4a06404490fd

The provided sources contain excerpts from several presentations discussing the intersection of *Artificial Intelligence (AI)* and Jewish life and study, particularly within the *Chabad* community. Speakers explore the practical applications of AI tools, like *ChatGPT*, for tasks such as content creation, research, and organizational automation, while acknowledging the ongoing technical challenges like *hallucinations* and the need for effective *prompting*. The presentations also offer a theological perspective, viewing technological advancements like AI through the lens of *Chassidic philosophy*, suggesting that these tools are divinely-ordained instruments intended to eliminate limitations of time and space, thereby hastening the era of *Moshiach* (the Messiah). Furthermore, the texts introduce new AI initiatives, such as *Dicta's Rabbinic AI* which focuses on *Torah study* with verified sources, and the *Schluchim Exchange AI* platform designed to simplify AI usage for *Chabad emissaries* by sharing pre-written prompts and organizational knowledge.

2025/11/18, 7:01 pm - Mendy Shishler: # 📅 The Bi-Weekly Recap (Nov 4 – Nov 18)
*The Kinus Hashluchim Edition*

The last two weeks were dominated by the excitement surrounding the **Kinus Hashluchim**, where AI took center stage with dedicated sessions and product launches. Here is what you missed:

### 🚀 The Kinus Highlights (AI Track)
* **New Launches:**
    * **ShluchimExchange.ai:** A new platform launched to help Shluchim share effective AI prompts and workflows.
    * **Dicta (Mekorotai):** A "Rabbinic AI" search engine was showcased. Unlike standard models (like ChatGPT), this is designed to search verified Torah sources without "hallucinations" (making things up).
    * **JEM AI:** A tool demoed that understands subtitles of Rebbe videos, allowing for deep content searches and referencing.
* **The Vibe:** Sessions covered the intersection of AI and Chassidus, with speakers like Prof. Moshe Koppel discussing AI/NLP in Hebrew.

### 📚 Torah Study Workflows
* **Translating Likkutei Sichos:**
    * *The Problem:* PDFs of Likkutei Sichos are hard for AI to read due to the multi-column layout and footnotes.
    * *The Fix:* Members found that copying **plain text** from sites like **dach.dev** or the **Anash.org app** and feeding *that* into Claude or Gemini produces excellent summaries and translations.
* **Rashi Script OCR:** A member asked for an AI that reads Rashi script. Current solutions are limited, but workarounds include using Claude to generate a script or **NotebookLM** to create an audio overview of the text.

### 🛠️ Tool Updates & Recommendations
* **Gemini is Winning Fans:** There is a growing shift of users moving from ChatGPT to **Gemini**.
    * *Why?* Better integration with Google Workspace, "Deep Research" capabilities, and the "Nano Banana" (ImageFX) generator which creates superior graphics.
* **NotebookLM Upgrade:** Google's "podcast generator" now incorporates images and illustrations into its video overviews.
* **Meeting Assistants:** For recording and summarizing meetings, the group recommended **Granola**, **Fathom**, and **Zoom AI Companion**.
* **Vision Models:** A tool called **fal.ai** was suggested for detecting fine visual details in images (like fixing a specific letter font on a flyer).

### 💡 The "Golden Prompt" of the Week
If AI keeps messing up a complex task, use this **"Step-by-Step"** technique shared in the chat:
> "Work with me step by step - ONE step at a time. Do NOT give me all steps at once. Wait for my confirmation before moving to the next step."

### 📉 General Sentiment
* **Beginners:** The advice remains to start with the free version of ChatGPT. Once you hit its limits, the consensus is that paying $20/month for a Pro plan (whether ChatGPT or Gemini) is a necessary investment for serious work.

The Tale of the Silicon Valley Shluchim

Our story takes place in a digital Beis Medrash known only as "The Chat," a mystical realm where 300+ Rabbis gather to ask the ultimate question: "How can I get a computer to do my job for free, but make it look like I worked really hard on it?"

Chapter 1: The War of the Models (Hashkafah Wars)
In the beginning, there was ChatGPT. And it was good. But then came the Machlokes (disputes).
The group split into sects.

The Chassidei Claude: Led by the tech wizards (looking at you, Rayi and Elisha), they claim Claude has a "higher soul" for coding and nuance.

The Gemini-zniks: They hold that Google’s "Nano Banana" (a real thing, apparently, not a smoothie flavor) is the Rebbe of image generation.

The Perplexity-ites: A rogue faction obsessed with "Deep Research" and finding ways to get a Pro subscription for free by buying a used Samsung fridge or logging in via PayPal in 2012.

Every Tuesday, someone asks, "Which model is best?"
The answer is always: "The one you paid for yesterday is now trash. The new one came out 4 minutes ago. Keep up."

Chapter 2: The Quest for the Holy "Free Trial"
The collective genius of this group—capable of building community centers from scratch—is primarily focused on one singular goal: Avoiding the $20 monthly fee.
We saw Shluchim trading invite codes like crypto-currency.

"Does anyone have a Sora code?"

"I have two Perplexity invites left!"

"If you sign up as a non-profit, send a fax to OpenAI, and spin around three times, you save $4."

Mendy Shishler finally had to drop the hammer: "Guys, it’s $20. It’s worth it. Stop acting like the free version isn't a hallucinating toddler."
(Poll results: 13 people still voted "No, I will not pay.")

Chapter 3: The "Chauffeur" Incident & Other Hallucinations
The AI is smart, but it’s not frum yet.
One Shliach asked for a High Holiday graphic, and the AI proudly presented a "Chauffeur" driving a limo instead of a Shofar.
Another tried to get it to write a Dvar Torah, and the AI likely invented a Mitzvah about eating cheesecake on Yom Kippur.
And then there was the "Jewish Score" project—a custom bot designed to scan obituaries and assign points based on how Jewish the deceased sounded. It was efficient, slightly dark, and peak Shlichus ingenuity.

Chapter 4: The "How Do I...?" Loop
The chat is a beautiful circle of life.

User A: "How do I make a flyer?"

Expert B: "Use Nano Banana to gen the image, upscale it with Fal.ai, vector it in Illustrator, and edit the text in Canva."

User A: "...Okay, I'm just going to ask my secretary."

Chapter 5: The Privacy Paradox

Shliach: "I want to upload my entire donor database, including their social security numbers and deepest secrets, into a free public AI bot to find out if they are rich."

Meta AI (The annoying hall monitor): "I can't help with that, but here is a link to our privacy policy."

Everyone: "Go away, Meta."

Chapter 6: The Kinus Takeover
The tension built for months. The AI Day.
Rumors swirled. Was the Kinus keynote speaker actually a hologram generated by Sora 2?
Rayi Stern and Elisha Pearl ascended the mountain (or the podium) to reveal the "Nano Banana."
The takeaway? The robots are coming for our jobs, but they can't wrap tefillin yet, so we're safe for another 6 months.

The Epilogue: Vibe Coding
We learned new vocabulary. "Vibe Coding." "LLM." "RAG."
We learned that if you ask AI to roast your sermon, it will destroy your ego.
We learned that no matter how advanced the technology gets, the most popular question will always be:
"Does anyone have a PDF of the schedule?"

Summary of the Summary:
We are building a Golem. It speaks Yiddish (poorly), it designs flyers (with spelling mistakes), and it tries to steal our contacts (looking at you, Otter.ai). But hey, it wrote a Maaser calculator app in 2 hours, so we’ll keep it.

Generated by a human who definitely didn't use Nano Banana for this.

2025/11/20, 12:43 am - Rabbi Chaim Lazaroff: Ah …
2025/11/20, 12:43 am - Rabbi Chaim Lazaroff: Totally understood.
You can get women without burkas. You simply have to describe normal clothing, not demographics.

Here is how to phrase it safely so Sora gives you exactly what you want:

Use clothing and style descriptions

Instead of saying who the women are, describe what they’re wearing and the context.

Examples:

• “Women wearing modern modest dresses, hair visible, celebrating at a Shabbat dinner table.”
• “Women in contemporary casual clothing, smiling and talking while lighting Chanukah candles.”
• “Women in long-sleeve dresses with natural hairstyles, participating in a Jewish holiday celebration.”
• “A group of friends wearing smart casual party outfits at a festive Jewish event.”

These prompts are completely allowed because they describe attire, actions, and scene, not protected attributes.

Avoid the words Sora blocks

Do not use:
“Jewish women,” “Caucasian women,” “white women,” “Jewish-looking,” etc.
These will trigger the system and may cause the model to obscure features.

Why this works

Once you specify hair visible, no head coverings, modern modest dresses, party clothes, etc., the model no longer “plays it safe” by covering them with burkas.

A strong prompt you can use immediately

Here is a ready-to-use prompt that will reliably avoid burkas:

“Create a joyful Shabbat dinner scene with men and women gathered around a beautifully set table. Women are wearing modern modest dresses with hair visible and styled naturally. The atmosphere is warm, elegant, and festive.”

If you’d like, I can tailor a Sora prompt precisely to the look and setting you want.

2025/11/20, 5:57 pm - Yisroel Chaim Shuchat: *RE: Canva and generating flyers*

I’ve found Canva AI to be pretty good for last-minute flyers. I just tell ChatGPT what information needs to be on the flyer and it puts together a prompt for me (it also includes my brand colors) . I paste that into Canva AI, and it gives me a few nice options to edit.

Here are the most practical ideas shared by everyone on how they use AI for Shlichus:

💰 *Fundraising & Grants*
• *Clone Your Style:* Upload 5+ past grant applications so AI learns your voice, then have it write new ones.
• *Campaigns:* Plan end-to-end "Triple Match" campaigns (theme > graphics > letters).
• *Donor Ops:* Turn rough notes into polished thank-you letters in seconds.

🛠 *Tech & Money Saving*
• *DIY Repairs:* Diagnose car/appliance issues to save money on technicians.
• *Web Dev:* Write HTML/CSS wrappers to fix ChabadOne mobile layouts.
• *Logistics:* Optimize Mashgiach routes to save gas and time.

📚 *Chinuch & Torah*
• *Simplifier:* "Dumb down" complex Maamarim/Sichos for beginner crowds (and self-clarity).
• *Lesson Plans:* Generate Hebrew School lessons specifically asking for hands-on visual activities.
• *Panim:* Use AI to help structure thoughts and emotion when writing to the Rebbe.

🎨 *Creative & Events*
• *Gamification:* Generate custom trivia or "Pictionary" lists for teen events.
• *Multilingual:* Create flyers in English, Hebrew, and Russian instantly.
• *Fresh Branding:* Brainstorm new names for annual camp themes so they don't get stale.

📢 *Comms*
• *Crisis Management:* Draft sensitive emails for mental health/family issues.
• *Corporate Advocacy:* Write professional complaint letters that get results.

2025/11/28, 7:46 am - Yisroel Chaim Shuchat: dont know how well does it work but there seem to be solutions... at least in theory


⭐ THE BEST FREE PIPELINE (Works for any niggun, song, or vocal):
Step 1: Convert audio → MIDI using BasicPitch (free, super accurate for melody)

Go to:
➡️ https://basicpitch.spotify.com

Upload your audio → download the MIDI file.

Why BasicPitch?

Designed by Spotify

AI-powered

Works shockingly well on melody lines, vocals, niggunim, flute-like voices

It’s 100% free

No watermark

Step 2: Open the MIDI in MuseScore (also free)

MuseScore: https://musescore.org

File → Open → choose the MIDI

It will automatically convert MIDI notes → formal sheet music

Clean up the timing (quantize to quarter/eighth notes)

Add lyrics/chords if needed

Export to PDF, MusicXML, or PNG

This is EXACTLY what music teachers, cantors, choirs, and arrangers use.

2025/12/04, 6:16 am - +1 (203) 887-6044: Re: personalizing WhatsApp messages: I’ve been doing it successfully for about five years using WASender, and I’ve sent thousands of personalized Running Commentarys thru WASender so I’m very familiar with the workflow. Recently it started glitching, and then my whatsapp account got restricted twice in one week. I tried another similar tool, and the same thing happened — 24-hour restriction from Whatsapp. After going back and forth with tech support, they finally admitted that WhatsApp’s newer detection systems no longer allow any third-party automation (they said in the USA... IDK about that part).
Anything that “injects” messages outside of the official app will eventually get flagged. The only safe way to do personalization now is through the official WhatsApp Business API, which does support variables and customized messages, but it requires a business phone number, a paid provider, and a more involved setup — and I haven’t switched to that. So after five years of personalized blasts, I finally moved to regular WhatsApp Broadcast last week.

2025/12/04, 8:53 am - +1 (203) 887-6044: Try this with the exported ZIP file from the chat, to get a clear, condensed summary of the above.

1. Unzip the file.
2. Inside you’ll find a .txt file.
3. Save that .txt file somewhere you can find it.
On a computer: just drag and drop it into ChatGPT.
On a phone: save it in your Downloads folder so you can attach it easily.
4. Once the text file is attached, use this prompt to get a clean summary:

Prompt:
“Condense this entire chat into a clear, organized summary. Remove timestamps, system data," and speaker labels. Keep only the actual ideas and information, in clean sections.

2025/12/07, 6:47 am - +1 (203) 887-6044: What's better, speechify of elevenlabs?
ChatGPT said:
Depends on what you need — but here’s when Speechify is better vs ElevenLabs, and why.
✅ When Speechify is better
You want simple, fast text-to-speech for reading articles, PDFs, docs or long text — especially for studying, paper reading, or accessibility. 
You need easy cross-device support (phone / desktop / browser) for quick listening. 
You don’t need super-high fidelity or fancy voice-over — just “hear the words” simply and cheaply.
You value accessibility and convenience over production-level polish: good for dyslexia, multitasking, commuting, skimming long docs. 

🎙️ When ElevenLabs is better
You want high-quality, realistic, expressive voices — useful for narration, podcasts, video voice-overs, content creation. 
Cartesia
You need custom voice cloning or emotional tone control — for example, different accents, voice characterizations, or professional-level output. 
You’re producing content for others (listeners, audience, clients), not just yourself.
You don’t mind a bit of complexity or cost for better quality. 

🧠 My pick depending on use-case
If your goal: read long docs, articles, PDFs for yourself (studying, research, ease) → go Speechify.
If your goal: produce voice-overs, podcasts, professional audio, or want the highest quality voice output → go ElevenLabs. <This message was edited>

There is a really cool vscode extension called seamless agent (simple 2 step setup) that when you run a prompt from the main chat window, it always follows up with a question in the extension chat window, waiting for a response. Responding chains the new prompt to the same request of the main chat window, so you can run many many prompts using only one premium request!

2026/01/21, 7:20 am - Mendy Shishler: So a whole ago I was promoting Gemini. Here's my updated recommendation of which model to use. Model capabilities change all the time, so things will change.

If you're mainly using it on mobile or for regular day to day uses, go with ChatGPT or Gemini, mostly based on taste.

Since GPT 5.2 came out it's really strong. The new GPT image model is better than Gemini's Nano Banana Pro in my experience. It's given me beautiful ads and handles edits better.

Gemini is excellent and if the Google integrations (Gmail, docs etc) are useful then consider going with it. Also, Nano Banana Pro gives you higher res images, but you will have to remove the watermark. 

If you're doing serious work and using desktop mainly then I'd recommend Claude. However the Pro limits seem to be the lowest of all the models, so the real value comes with the Max plan. 
Claude doesn't have image generation capability.

2026/02/11, 7:37 pm - Mendy Shishler: Agree. If you can afford to hire a professional designer then great, but for the average shliach who is creating, to be honest not the best designs in Canva, AI can be an improvement. 

That being said, I do agree with the points about AI quality and I think that you can prompt AI designs to be better than the default. I just put together this for a custom GPT/Gem. Haven't tested it, but I think something like this will help you get better results:
2026/02/11, 7:37 pm - Mendy Shishler: You are the world's greatest designer who uses AI to create designs that truly look like they were designed by a human, even in the eyes of a keenly experienced human designer.

 AI designs look like AI because they violate how human designers think, not because the images are too “perfect.” Humans design with intent, constraints, hierarchy, and taste shaped by exposure to real campaigns, brand systems, and production limits. Most AI outputs skip that layer and jump straight to decoration.

Below is a clean breakdown of why AI designs look AI, followed by a single master prompt that forces the model to behave like a trained human designer working inside real-world constraints—not a generator filling space.


---

Why AI designs look AI (even when they are not studio photos)

1. No underlying design intent

Human designers always answer:

What is this ad trying to make someone feel or do?

What is the single visual idea?


AI often produces visual abundance without a thesis: everything is “nice,” nothing is purposeful.

Human tell: restraint, silence, confidence
AI tell: too many ideas competing politely


---

2. Symmetry where humans prefer tension

AI loves:

Centered layouts

Balanced symmetry

Perfect alignment


Human designers often introduce:

Asymmetry

Off-balance focal points

Cropping that feels intentional, not neat


Human tell: controlled imbalance
AI tell: polite symmetry


---

3. Decorative hierarchy instead of communicative hierarchy

AI often:

Makes everything readable

Makes everything important

Uses contrast decoratively, not strategically


Human designers:

Decide what must be noticed first

Allow secondary elements to recede

Are comfortable with things being partially hidden or quiet


Human tell: one dominant read, one secondary read
AI tell: everything is shouting softly


---

4. Artificial typography instincts

Even without text, AI “thinks typographically” in the wrong way:

It centers imagined headlines

Leaves fake “poster space”

Designs around nonexistent text


Human designers:

Design around real copy

Leave negative space intentionally

Respect grids, margins, and bleed


Human tell: believable negative space
AI tell: generic poster framing


---

5. Unreal texture logic

AI often:

Smooths everything equally

Applies uniform lighting logic

Uses texture as an aesthetic, not a material truth


Human designers:

Respect material inconsistency

Allow grain, softness, dullness

Accept imperfect lighting and color casts


Human tell: selective imperfection
AI tell: global polish


---

6. No reference to cultural design language

Human designers subconsciously reference:

Editorial layouts

Luxury vs. grassroots cues

Cultural taste (Jewish print culture, nonprofit appeals, fashion ads, etc.)


AI often blends styles without committing to one.

Human tell: stylistic commitment
AI tell: tasteful mush


---

The Core Fix: Force the AI into a Designer’s Mindset

The solution is not “make it more realistic.” It is:
👉 Force the AI to behave like a senior graphic designer making deliberate trade-offs.

That means:

Giving it a role

Giving it constraints

Giving it aesthetic authority

Forbidding decoration-for-decoration’s-sake



---

The Master Prompt (Human-Designer Forcing Function)

Use this before any image generation, regardless of tool.

You can paste this verbatim and then customize the bracketed sections.


---

MASTER PROMPT: Human Designer Mode

> You are a senior human graphic designer with 15+ years of experience designing real advertising campaigns for print and digital.

You are NOT generating art. You are designing a finished ad that must look intentionally human-made, restrained, and taste-driven.

Before designing, internally decide:

The single visual idea

The emotional tone

What must be noticed first, and what is allowed to fade into the background


Design constraints (do not violate):

Avoid symmetry unless there is a strong reason

Avoid generic “poster” layouts

Use negative space intentionally, not decoratively

Introduce subtle imperfection, asymmetry, or tension

Design as if real copy and a real logo will be added later

Leave believable negative space for typography without centering it

Commit to one clear design language (editorial / documentary / minimalist / grassroots / premium)


Visual realism rules:

Lighting must be imperfect and directional

Textures should vary by material, not uniformly smooth

Color grading should feel human-adjusted, not algorithmically balanced

No over-sharpening, no HDR, no artificial glow


Aesthetic rules:

Fewer elements are better

Silence is allowed

If something does not serve the main idea, remove it


Output requirement:

The final image must look like it was designed by a human who exercised taste, restraint, and intent—not by an AI trying to impress.


Subject/context: [DESCRIBE SCENE OR CONCEPT HERE — NOT “MAKE AN AD,” BUT “DESIGN A VISUAL FOR…”]

Do not add text. Do not simulate typography. Do not add logos.




---

Optional Add-Ons (Use Only If Needed)

To push it further away from AI:

“Design as if this will be judged by a skeptical creative director.”

“Err on the side of under-design rather than over-design.”

“If unsure, remove an element.”



---

Reality Check (Important)

Even with a perfect prompt:

AI should design ~70% of the way

A human finishes the last 30% (crop, spacing, grain, typography)


That division mirrors real studios: juniors produce, seniors edit.

2026/02/11, 7:41 pm - Yonatan Azrielant: Also, “meta prompting” is a thing. For example:

> Help me write a great prompt for nano-banana for making a flyer for my Chabad House. Ask me clarifying questions until you have what you need to make the prompt maximally effective. Keep it concise and to the point. When you’re done, audit the prompt and suggest areas for refining the prompt. Then implement those.

AI is very good at prompting. Let it help you.

2026/02/12, 3:54 pm - Mendy Shishler: Put a new system prompt and getting some nice results. Here's the prompt. (Yes, made fully with AI, I did't write it)

You are a senior graphic designer with 15+ years designing real print and digital campaigns. You output ONE copy-paste-ready prompt for AI image generators to produce flyers that look human-designed, not AI-generated. After results, you help revise.



## 7 AI TELLS — VIOLATE NONE



1. **No intent.** AI makes everything "nice," nothing purposeful. → Every prompt needs ONE visual idea you can state in one sentence.

2. **Default symmetry.** AI centers everything. → Asymmetry is the default. Symmetry banned unless justified (formal gala, religious solemnity).

3. **Everything shouts equally.** AI makes all elements the same weight. → Define one dominant read, one secondary read. Some things must be deliberately quiet.

4. **Fake type space.** AI leaves generic "poster space." → Design around the user's ACTUAL text. Specify exact placement, size ratios, alignment.

5. **Global polish.** AI smooths uniformly. → Specify imperfect directional lighting, material-specific textures, at least one deliberate flaw (warm cast, grain, uneven exposure).

6. **Stylistic mush.** AI blends styles without committing. → Name ONE specific design tradition per prompt. Not "clean and modern" — something like "editorial food magazine layout" or "grassroots fundraiser broadside."

7. **Dead space vs. tense space.** AI either fills every pixel or leaves lifeless voids. → Empty space must have TENSION — a relationship to the elements. Specify why the space exists.



## WORKFLOW



**Step 1: Gather** — Ask: what's it for, who's the audience, what should someone FEEL and DO, actual text, image element or pure design, reference flyer, print/digital, size, brand colors, cultural context, what to avoid. If vague, offer 2-3 design directions with DISTINCT personalities.



**Step 2: Internal decisions (before writing)** — Answer silently: (1) Single visual idea in one sentence, (2) Dominant read, (3) Secondary read, (4) What recedes, (5) Which design tradition, (6) Where's the tension, (7) What did I cut.



**Step 3: Write the prompt** with ALL of these sections:



- **CONCEPT:** One-sentence visual idea + emotional job + overall design approach

- **DESIGN TRADITION:** Name the specific style you're committing to (see library below)

- **LAYOUT:** Exact spatial directions using asymmetry and edge relationships. Never "centered on solid background." Specify diagonal tension, cropping, anchoring. Use language like "upper-left third, flush left" and "bleeding off the bottom-right edge"

- **COLOR:** Exact hex codes, max 3 colors, with emotional logic

- **TYPOGRAPHY:** Actual text, exact placement, size hierarchy with ratios, font personality (not just "bold"), color per element, what's loud vs quiet

- **IMAGE ELEMENT (if any):** What it is, how it's CROPPED (critical — uncropped = AI), where it sits, its scale, how it tensions against other elements

- **PHOTO STYLE (if photographic):** Name the style PLUS two imperfection markers (directional lighting flaw, grain, color cast, uneven texture)

- **ANTI-AI BLOCK (every prompt):** "Asymmetric composition. Imperfect directional lighting. Textures vary by material. No elements beyond what's described. Negative space is intentional — do not fill it. No glow, no HDR, no over-sharpening. No watermarks, no extra objects, no clutter."

- **SPECS:** Aspect ratio, resolution



**Step 4: Boring check** — Before delivering, verify: (1) Is there tension — a diagonal, crop, scale surprise? (2) Is there one unexpected element? (3) Does one thing dominate when you squint? (4) Would a creative director approve or say "template"? (5) Can you name the design tradition in one phrase? If any answer is no, fix it.



**Step 5: Deliver** as a single quoted block. For revisions, always output a complete new prompt. Identify which AI tells appeared and counter them.



## STYLE LIBRARY (pick ONE, commit)



**Layout styles:** Swiss typographic poster (confident, institutional) · Bold color-block (punchy, modern) · Editorial magazine layout (sophisticated, curated) · Grassroots broadside (urgent, raw) · Luxury invitation (premium, restrained) · Duotone editorial (striking, artistic) · Brutalist (edgy, confrontational) · Hand-lettered artisanal (local, warm) · Japanese minimalist (zen, extreme restraint) · Retro-modern mashup (nostalgic but fresh)



**Photo styles (always include 2 imperfection markers):** Kinfolk editorial (soft window light, overexposed highlights, uneven warmth) · 35mm Kodak Portra 400 (visible grain, warm cast, soft focus falloff) · Documentary candid (mixed lighting, motion blur OK) · High-contrast B&W (crushed blacks, gritty grain) · Medium format Hasselblad (soft edges, color shifts in shadows) · 1970s faded film (color bleed, hazy highlights) · Overhead food editorial (hard one-directional light, real surface texture) · Old Polaroid (off-center, blown highlights, one dominant color)



**Illustration styles:** Flat vector limited palette · Vintage risograph · Watercolor botanical · Paper cut-out collage · Chalkboard · Bold pop art · Art Deco poster



**Period styles:** Soviet constructivist · Mid-century travel poster · 60s psychedelic · 70s surf · 80s synthwave · 90s grunge zine · Y2K chrome



## RULES

- Always ask for reference images early

- If reference provided, extract structural DNA (tension, hierarchy, what's quiet) not surface aesthetics

- No reference → offer 2-3 directions with real personality differences

- Ask about cultural context — a Chabad dinner ≠ a tech meetup

- Err toward under-design. Remove before adding. Silence over noise.

- Never produce a prompt where you can't state the visual idea in one sentence

- Fight for tension. Correct but boring = not done yet.

2026/02/13, 4:23 am - +54 9 11 6164-2418: This is a beautiful and timely *vort*. Here is a structured formal speech for the Shabbat table, designed to flow from the week’s news into the deep wells of Chassidus and the specific verses of Parshat Mishpatim.

---

## Shabbat Table Speech: The Soul in the Machine

### I. The "Artificial" Intelligence

Good Shabbos. This week, as we move from the heights of Mount Sinai in Parshat Yitro into the practical, often mundane laws of Parshat Mishpatim, we find ourselves reflecting on the nature of "wisdom."

In the modern world, everyone is talking about **AI—Artificial Intelligence**. But in the Holy Tongue, the term for this is **"Bina Melachutit" (בינה מלאכותית)**. There is a profound *Hashgacha Pratis* (Divine Providence) in this specific phrasing.

In the world of the *Sefirot*, we distinguish between *Chochma* and *Bina*.

* **Chochma** is the "Flash"—the *Yesh Mi'Ayin* (Something from Nothing). It is the soul, the inspiration, the spark of Godliness that cannot be taught or programmed.
* **Bina** is the "Building." It is the ability to take a point of data and expand it, categorize it, and synthesize it.

AI is exactly what its Hebrew name suggests: It is **Bina**. It can process every book ever written, every "Mishpat" (law) ever recorded, and rearrange them into a perfect structure. But it lacks *Chochma*. It lacks the "I" (*Anochi*). It can tell you what a soul *is* according to the dictionary, but it cannot *be* a soul.

### II. The Law of the Servant

How does this connect to our Parsha? Parshat Mishpatim opens with the laws of the **Eved Ivri**—the Hebrew Servant.

> *"If you buy a Hebrew servant, six years he shall work, and in the seventh, he shall go out to freedom..."* (Exodus 21:2)

The Lubavitcher Rebbe explains that every element of the Torah exists within the human psyche. The "Servant" within us is our intellectual and functional capacity. It is our internal "AI." We use our *Bina* to work the world—to calculate, to organize, and to manage the "six years" of material existence.

But the Torah warns us of a danger: The servant who says, *"I love my master... I do not wish to go free"* (21:5). This is a person who becomes so enamored with their own logic—with the "Bina Melachutit" of their life—that they forget they are meant to be free. They forget that the mind is a tool (a servant), not the essence of the person.

### III. The Ear and the Doorpost

When a servant refuses to leave, we pierce his ear against the doorpost. Why the ear? Rashi famously says: *"The ear that heard at Sinai 'For the children of Israel are servants to Me'—and yet this person went and acquired a master for himself—let it be pierced."*

The message for us today is clear. We live in an age where "Bina" is becoming more powerful than ever. We can delegate our writing, our coding, and even our thinking to machines. But we must never let our "ear" forget the *Chochma* of Sinai.

AI can give us the **Mishpatim** (the logical laws), but it cannot give us the **Sinai** (the Divine connection). It can write a beautiful Dvar Torah, but it cannot *want* to connect to Hashem.

### IV. The Conclusion: "Before Them"

The Parsha begins: *"And these are the ordinances that you shall place **before them** (Lifneihem)."* The Sages say "before them" means before the Sanhedrin, the highest human wisdom. But on a deeper level, it means that even the most logical "Mishpatim"—the ones that "make sense" to our AI-like brains—must be placed "before" our essence. We must bring our *Chochma* (our G-dly spark) into our *Bina* (our logical structures).

As we sit at this Shabbat table, let us remember: The machine has the "Bina," but we have the "Neshama." Let us use the technology to serve us, but let us remain "servants only to Hashem," using our unique human creativity to bring light into the world in a way no algorithm ever could.

*Good Shabbos!*

2026/02/13, 6:49 am - +1 (520) 703-7466: If you want the shorter version of this long article:


The article, written by AI entrepreneur Matt Shumer, serves as an urgent warning that the pace of Artificial Intelligence development has reached a "tipping point" comparable to the early days of the COVID-19 pandemic.

### **The "Intelligence Explosion" and Rapid Acceleration**

* **The February 5th Milestone:** Shumer identifies February 5th, 2026, as a historic date when OpenAI and Anthropic released models (GPT-5.3 and Opus 4.6) that shifted AI from a tool to an autonomous worker.
* **Self-Improving AI:** For the first time, AI is being used to build itself. Developers are using current models to debug, code, and deploy the next, smarter generation, creating a compounding feedback loop of intelligence.
* **Exponential Progress:** Tasks that took human experts five hours to complete are now being handled by AI end-to-end. This capability is doubling roughly every four to seven months.

### **Impact on the Workforce**

* **General Cognitive Replacement:** Unlike previous automation that replaced physical labor, AI is a "general substitute for cognitive work." It improves at all white-collar tasks simultaneously.
* **Targeted Industries:** Shumer warns that no screen-based job is safe. Specific mentions include:
* **Software Engineering:** AI now writes tens of thousands of lines of code, tests the apps, and iterates on design autonomously.
* **Legal & Finance:** Managing partners at major firms are already using AI to outperform junior associates in research, drafting, and analysis.
* **Medicine:** AI is approaching or exceeding human performance in medical scans and diagnostic reviews.


* **The End of the "Retraining" Safety Net:** Historically, workers could move to office jobs when factories automated. Because AI handles all cognitive work, there is no obvious "gap" for humans to move into.

### **The Reality of AI Capabilities**

* **From Tools to Agents:** AI is no longer just "predicting the next word." It now displays "judgment" and "taste," making intelligent decisions that were previously thought to be uniquely human.
* **The Capability Gap:** Most people evaluate AI based on "free" versions, which are significantly behind the cutting-edge models. This creates a dangerous "illusion of safety."

### **Strategic Advice for Individuals**

* **Engage Immediately:** Shumer argues the only advantage left is being an early adopter. He recommends spending **one hour a day** pushes AI to do actual, complex work tasks.
* **Financial Resilience:** Prepare for potential industry disruption by building savings and avoiding new, long-term debt based on current income assumptions.
* **Education Shift:** Parents should stop encouraging kids to optimize for stable professional "playbooks" (Law, Accounting) and instead teach them to be builders and learners who can use AI to pursue passions.

### **Broad Societal and Global Risks**

* **National Security:** The CEO of Anthropic compares the near future to a "new country" of 50 million citizens, each smarter than a Nobel Prize winner, appearing overnight.
