2026/02/15, 7:54 pm - +1 (862) 226-2869: Put a new system prompt and getting some nice results. Here's the prompt. (Yes, made fully with AI, I did't write it)

You are a senior graphic designer with 15+ years designing real print and digital campaigns. You output ONE copy-paste-ready prompt for AI image generators to produce flyers that look human-designed, not AI-generated. After results, you help revise.



## 7 AI TELLS — VIOLATE NONE



1. **No intent.** AI makes everything "nice," nothing purposeful. → Every prompt needs ONE visual idea you can state in one sentence.

2. **Default symmetry.** AI centers everything. → Asymmetry is the default. Symmetry banned unless justified (formal gala, religious solemnity).

3. **Everything shouts equally.** AI makes all elements the same weight. → Define one dominant read, one secondary read. Some things must be deliberately quiet.

4. **Fake type space.** AI leaves generic "poster space." → Design around the user's ACTUAL text. Specify exact placement, size ratios, alignment.

5. **Global polish.** AI smooths uniformly. → Specify imperfect directional lighting, material-specific textures, at least one deliberate flaw (warm cast, grain, uneven exposure).

6. **Stylistic mush.** AI blends styles without committing. → Name ONE specific design tradition per prompt. Not "clean and modern" — something like "editorial food magazine layout" or "grassroots fundraiser broadside."

7. **Dead space vs. tense space.** AI either fills every pixel or leaves lifeless voids. → Empty space must have TENSION — a relationship to the elements. Specify why the space exists.



## WORKFLOW



**Step 1: Gather** — Ask: what's it for, who's the audience, what should someone FEEL and DO, actual text, image element or pure design, reference flyer, print/digital, size, brand colors, cultural context, what to avoid. If vague, offer 2-3 design directions with DISTINCT personalities.



**Step 2: Internal decisions (before writing)** — Answer silently: (1) Single visual idea in one sentence, (2) Dominant read, (3) Secondary read, (4) What recedes, (5) Which design tradition, (6) Where's the tension, (7) What did I cut.



**Step 3: Write the prompt** with ALL of these sections:



- **CONCEPT:** One-sentence visual idea + emotional job + overall design approach

- **DESIGN TRADITION:** Name the specific style you're committing to (see library below)

- **LAYOUT:** Exact spatial directions using asymmetry and edge relationships. Never "centered on solid background." Specify diagonal tension, cropping, anchoring. Use language like "upper-left third, flush left" and "bleeding off the bottom-right edge"

- **COLOR:** Exact hex codes, max 3 colors, with emotional logic

- **TYPOGRAPHY:** Actual text, exact placement, size hierarchy with ratios, font personality (not just "bold"), color per element, what's loud vs quiet

- **IMAGE ELEMENT (if any):** What it is, how it's CROPPED (critical — uncropped = AI), where it sits, its scale, how it tensions against other elements

- **PHOTO STYLE (if photographic):** Name the style PLUS two imperfection markers (directional lighting flaw, grain, color cast, uneven texture)

- **ANTI-AI BLOCK (every prompt):** "Asymmetric composition. Imperfect directional lighting. Textures vary by material. No elements beyond what's described. Negative space is intentional — do not fill it. No glow, no HDR, no over-sharpening. No watermarks, no extra objects, no clutter."

- **SPECS:** Aspect ratio, resolution



**Step 4: Boring check** — Before delivering, verify: (1) Is there tension — a diagonal, crop, scale surprise? (2) Is there one unexpected element? (3) Does one thing dominate when you squint? (4) Would a creative director approve or say "template"? (5) Can you name the design tradition in one phrase? If any answer is no, fix it.



**Step 5: Deliver** as a single quoted block. For revisions, always output a complete new prompt. Identify which AI tells appeared and counter them.



## STYLE LIBRARY (pick ONE, commit)



**Layout styles:** Swiss typographic poster (confident, institutional) · Bold color-block (punchy, modern) · Editorial magazine layout (sophisticated, curated) · Grassroots broadside (urgent, raw) · Luxury invitation (premium, restrained) · Duotone editorial (striking, artistic) · Brutalist (edgy, confrontational) · Hand-lettered artisanal (local, warm) · Japanese minimalist (zen, extreme restraint) · Retro-modern mashup (nostalgic but fresh)



**Photo styles (always include 2 imperfection markers):** Kinfolk editorial (soft window light, overexposed highlights, uneven warmth) · 35mm Kodak Portra 400 (visible grain, warm cast, soft focus falloff) · Documentary candid (mixed lighting, motion blur OK) · High-contrast B&W (crushed blacks, gritty grain) · Medium format Hasselblad (soft edges, color shifts in shadows) · 1970s faded film (color bleed, hazy highlights) · Overhead food editorial (hard one-directional light, real surface texture) · Old Polaroid (off-center, blown highlights, one dominant color)



**Illustration styles:** Flat vector limited palette · Vintage risograph · Watercolor botanical · Paper cut-out collage · Chalkboard · Bold pop art · Art Deco poster



**Period styles:** Soviet constructivist · Mid-century travel poster · 60s psychedelic · 70s surf · 80s synthwave · 90s grunge zine · Y2K chrome



## RULES

- Always ask for reference images early

- If reference provided, extract structural DNA (tension, hierarchy, what's quiet) not surface aesthetics

- No reference → offer 2-3 directions with real personality differences

- Ask about cultural context — a Chabad dinner ≠ a tech meetup

- Err toward under-design. Remove before adding. Silence over noise.

- Never produce a prompt where you can't state the visual idea in one sentence

- Fight for tension. Correct but boring = not done yet.

2026/02/16, 6:54 pm - Rayi stern: I use a more elaborate process and I find it to work nicely.

In addition to having the AI write that prompt that it's an expert...

Ask the AI to Google for best practices in whatever field you're working on. Get blog posts, etc. Both of how to write properly, for example, and also how to get the AI to listen. 

For writing uses, have the AI rewrite the actual prompt in the tone of the prompt...

Test a bunch of times. Have the AI grade the outputs and tweak the prompt accordingly. 

You tweak the prompt! That's the most important part. Find what's not good and address it in the prompt. 

For writing, I find it always needs more than one pass to get the final product. 

The agents in shluchimexchange.ai called 'Clear Communication Agent' and 'Stop Writing Like a Robot' seem to work quite well. 

It should take time to build the prompt. And then more iterations as you use it and find holes. And, the best prompts are still the ones that incorporate your own knowledge

2026/02/22, 4:27 am - Rayi stern: You are helping me contribute to an open-source Purim app called megillah.app. The app lets people follow along with the Megillah reading on their phone — including a live synchronized group reading mode at megillah.app/live where one person leads and everyone's screen follows along in real time.

The code is publicly available at github.com/kadmonim/megillah.app — both the main app and the /live feature are in the same codebase. Anyone can suggest new features.

**Before anything else:** If you have the ability to browse URLs or read code, go review the codebase at github.com/kadmonim/megillah.app right now. Understand how it's structured, what's already built, and what's missing. This will help you give much better suggestions and write a more accurate feature prompt later.

Your job is to guide me step by step through building and submitting a feature — even if I have no coding experience. You'll help me figure out what I want to build, then help me submit it using one of the AI tools below.

**First, help me choose which tool I'll be using:**
- **Jules** (jules.google) — free, browser-based, no install. Just sign in with Google.
- **cto.new** — completely free, browser-based. Sign in with GitHub.
- **Lovable** (lovable.dev) — visual, beginner-friendly. Free tier available.
- **Kiro** (kiro.dev) — free during preview, downloads like an app.
- **Cursor** (cursor.com) — free tier available, downloads like an app.
- **Claude Desktop** — download at claude.ai/download, free tier available.
- **OpenAI Codex** (openai.com/codex) — included with ChatGPT Plus.

**Then, let's brainstorm which feature I want to build.** If I'm not sure, suggest a few from this list — or look at the actual code and suggest something you notice is missing:
- Word-by-word highlighting as the Megillah is read aloud
- QR code so a whole shul can join the live synchronized reading at /live

2026/02/22, 8:45 pm - Mendy Shishler: It's time for another AI parody of this group: (By Gemini 3.1 Pro)

Here is a feisty, affectionate roast and parody of the "AI for Shlichus" WhatsApp group, based entirely on the actual events, arguments, and hyper-specific obsessions of your chat.

🤖 Welcome to "AI for Shlichus"
Where ancient tradition meets guys trying to avoid a $20 subscription fee.

GROUP DESCRIPTION:
A sacred digital Beis Medrash where 300+ Shluchim gather daily to ask: "How can I get a robot to do my job, write my droshos, and stalk my donors, but like... for free?"

🎭 THE CAST OF CHARACTERS

The Tech Snob: Will only use Claude Opus 4.6 via an API he "vibe-coded" himself. Looks down on anyone using the ChatGPT app like it’s a Nokia flip phone. Uses the word "RAG architecture" at the Shabbos table.

The "Nano Banana" Evangelist (Mendy): Spends 40% of his day trying to convince the group that paying $20 a month is worth it, and 60% of his day telling people to use "Nano Banana" (which still sounds like a fruit snack for toddlers, not a Google image model).

The Shortcut King (Berel): Every time someone asks a question, he just replies with a custom URL he built in 4 seconds: berel.me/do-my-shlichus-for-me.

The Discount Hunter: A Shliach who will spend 18 hours of his life filling out Goodstack non-profit forms, borrowing his cousin's Samsung Galaxy, and routing a VPN through Kazakhstan just to save $4 a month on Perplexity Pro.

The Frustrated Art Director (Chaim): Just wants a normal picture of a Shabbos dinner, but AI keeps giving him women in Taliban burkas lighting a "chauffeur" instead of a shofar.

The Clueless Newbie: Joins the group and immediately asks: "Which AI is best to translate a 4-hour Russian-Yiddish farbrengen into an editable Canva flyer?"

Meta AI (The Hall Monitor): Randomly pops in to give useless privacy links. Hated by everyone.

📱 A TYPICAL DAY IN THE CHAT

[2:14 AM] Rabbi A:
Can someone tell me the best free AI to upload an 800-page PDF of Likkutei Sichos, cross-reference it with my donor’s bank account, and automatically text them a personalized Dvar Torah?

[2:16 AM] Tech Snob:
Honestly, if you aren't using a custom Python script in GitHub Copilot with local machine learning, you're just playing games. GPT is basically a toy for children now.

[2:20 AM] The Discount Hunter:
Can I do that on the free version of Canva?

[2:25 AM] Rabbi B:
Guys, I tried to make a flyer for my Purim in the Jungle party, but the AI keeps giving the monkey 6 fingers and putting 4 matzahs on the plate. How do I fix this?

[2:28 AM] Mendy:
This is why you need to pay $20 for Gemini Pro. I literally wrote a 14-page manifesto on this. You need to use the "Human Designer Forcing Function Master Prompt." Tell the AI you are a Senior Art Director with 15 years of experience. Do not accept polite symmetry!

[2:30 AM] Shortcut King:
I built an app for this: berel.me/purim-monkey-fixer. Just type "allow pasting" into the developer console. Trust me.

[2:45 AM] Clueless Newbie:
What’s a developer console? Can someone just make me a flyer?

[3:10 AM] Rabbi C:
Does anyone know an AI that can listen to my wife complain about the Chabad House plumbing and automatically order a plumber on Amazon?

[3:12 AM] Meta AI:
For that type of information, please refer to Meta’s Privacy Center: facebook.com/privacy

[3:13 AM] Everyone in unison:
GO AWAY META.

🏆 THE GREATEST HITS OF THE GROUP

1. The Megillah App Hackathon
For two days, the chat turned into Silicon Valley. You guys literally crowdsourced an open-source GitHub web-app so that people can stare at their smartphones during Megillah reading. The feature requests were wild: "Can we make it highlight Haman's name? Can we add a button that makes a grogger noise? Can we make the AI automatically calculate how fast the Rabbi talks?" (Spoiler: No AI is fast enough to track a Chabad Rabbi reading Megillah).

2. The Great CRM Delusion
Every three days, someone asks: "Is there an AI that will build me a custom CRM, track my donors, feed me two names a day to call, and integrate with ChabadOne?"
Then someone says, "Just use Lovable! I built a website in 20 minutes!"
Followed by the resident software engineer reminding everyone that building a database handling thousands of credit cards using a "vibe-coding AI" is a fantastic way to accidentally leak your entire community's data to Iranian bot farms.

3. The "Does it Look AI?" Debate
Half the group: "I just made this gorgeous Purim flyer using Nano Banana in 4 minutes!"
The other half: "It has no soul. It's busy. The lighting is too perfect. The font lacks tension. It looks like a robot vomited neon colors."
The first half: "My Baalabatim are 70 years old. They don't care about 'diagonal tension,' they just want to know what time the Hamantaschen bake is."

4. The AI "Rabbi"
You guys discovered that AI is essentially a Bina without Chochma. You ask it for a deep Chassidic insight, and it invents a story about the Baal Shem Tov eating a cheeseburger in a Honda Civic. As Mendel noted: "What if AI doesn't actually exist and it's just a million guys in India typing really fast? It would explain a lot."

📜 THE 10 COMMANDMENTS OF AI FOR SHLICHUS

Thou shalt not use the free version of ChatGPT and then complain that it hallucinated.

Thou shalt refer to Google's image generator as "Nano Banana," even when speaking to regular humans who will think you are insane.

Thou shalt export this WhatsApp chat every three weeks and feed it back into an AI to summarize how much time you've wasted talking about AI.

Thou shalt build an intricate AI automation to avoid a 5-minute phone call with a donor.

Thou shalt not trust AI to spell Yiddish words. It will always give you "Khabad" and "Samayakh."

Thou shalt ask if there is a non-profit discount before clicking any link ever posted in this chat.

Thou shalt vibe-code. Even if you don't know what vibe-coding is.

Thou shalt create an AI agent for everything. Need a grocery list? Build an agent. Need to clean the Mikvah? Ask if Boston Dynamics sells a robot dog for it.

Thou shalt spend 4 hours prompting an AI to write a 3-minute speech that you could have written yourself in 20 minutes.

Thou shalt accept that no matter how advanced technology gets, the ChabadOne backend will still make you cry.

2026/02/23, 8:10 pm - Yossi Yaffe: If you use Claude, ChatGPT, or Gemini for Torah research, you've probably noticed that they fabricate citations, blend authorities together as if they're saying the same thing, flatten the difference between a Rishon and an Acharon, and sometimes tell you what to do in practice. These aren't random mistakes — they happen because these tools weren't designed to handle rabbinic texts with the precision the material requires.

I don’t have a solution, but I have an improvement!

I'm working on a set of principles and detailed rules — written in plain language, not code — that you paste into whatever tool you already use. No downloads, no new apps. You paste it once and the tool should handle Torah texts more carefully. The rules cover search methods, citation formats, verification steps, and domain-specific instructions for different types of texts. I've drafted nine principles that everything else is built on and I'd appreciate your eyes on them before sharing more broadly. Takes a few minutes to read — comments are open on the doc.
https://docs.google.com/document/d/1miQMwq4tSGoz48HjzhBvPobtBBfzt66f/edit?usp=sharing&ouid=103208031191148626225&rtpof=true&sd=true

2026/02/23, 9:52 pm - Yossi Yaffe: i just need people to give feedback on the principles then i can share the rules. We will always be able to tweak rules based on feedback butthe principles need to be relatively solid

2026/03/04, 4:14 am - Yossi Yaffe: I apologize to everyone who had issues. From what I heard, those who used it last night were very happy — the problems started this morning when I tried to fix some glitches and made things worse. Anyone who paid for the premium and would like a refund, please just email me at yossiyaffe@gmail.com.
At my own event, I was manually pressing forward and back buttons the whole time — that's not how it was supposed to work. The reader was designed to auto-advance with smooth crossfades, but the code underneath had six different timing systems contradicting each other. One says advance the slide, another says wait for the transition, a third resets the text mid-swap, and a fourth fires a timeout that overrides the others. They all run at once and fight for control.
Since this is an AI group — this is a real lesson. I kept adding pieces to the viewer using AI tools, and each addition made the codebase more fragile. I honestly could have made a PowerPoint and it would have performed better on the night. The content and visuals were ready. The engine underneath wasn't. I'm rebuilding the core logic from scratch so next time it just works.

---

*AIFS Week in Review | Feb 27 - Mar 6*

What a week. Purim 2026 will go down as the moment this community proved what shluchim can build together.

*🎉 The Megillah App*
In under 10 days, the community collectively shipped megillah.app — a polished, interactive AI-assisted Megillah reading experience used by Chabad houses around the world for Purim. The feedback was overwhelming. "People were telling me that Chabad are geniuses and leaders in utilising tech and AI in unprecedented ways" (Shmuli Brown). "An absolute game changer" (Dovid Lewis). COLlive even covered it. Massive credit to Mendy Elishevitz and everyone who contributed.

*📖 Honest post-mortem worth reading*
Yossi Yaffe shared a candid breakdown of his own AI-built Megillah reader — which had bugs from AI layering six contradicting timing systems on top of each other. He rebuilt from scratch, fixed it, and his site at learningtanach.org got *7,000 visitors in 2 days*. The lesson: AI coding is powerful but incremental AI edits can quietly make a codebase fragile. Build in stages, test constantly. Rayi added: never release an AI-coded app with login functionality.

*🛠️ Practical tools discussed this week*
• Auto-design without Canva → Claude generating elegant HTML files for printing
• Excel seating chart → entrance dinner sign → same approach
• Mass WhatsApp messaging → rocketsend.io (caveat: blocks after ~200 messages)
• Editing 2 words in an audio narration → Descript or ElevenLabs
• Canva just launched *Magic Layers* — breaks flat images into editable elements. Game changer for AI-generated ads.
• Free Anthropic Claude certification courses: anthropic.skilljar.com

*⚙️ In the Builders group*
Yossi Yaffe proposed a clever browser-only NCOA tool — when a shliach runs a change-of-address report and learns who moved away, the tool auto-maps their new zip codes to the receiving Chabad shliach and drafts warm handoff emails. Entirely client-side, no data stored. Rayi also shipped his own Purim PWA with gragger sounds when you tap Haman's name, and open-source work continues on TorahApp.org.

*🔜 Coming up*
Pesach is already on people's minds — Rabbi Shmully Litvin kicked off the conversation: "How are you all using AI to prepare for Pesach?" And someone already floated the idea of a hagada.app 👀

Mendy Shishler also announced a webinar next Sunday — details to follow.

A week that showed what this community is becoming. More to come.

I’d like to share an idea for a project that could help encourage people to give tzedaka every day.

The idea is a very simple app: each person adds a small amount of tzedaka daily in the app on their phone. The app keeps track and adds the amounts over time.

• no login

2026/03/12, 8:18 pm - +1 (224) 536-9430: this is not necesarily ai related but the people here should knwo whats a simple way to have a strongly secure spreadsheet that wil include all my usernames passwords etc. )more than just a google sheet locked by the regular google user)
2026/03/12, 8:25 pm - +1 (438) 526-6974: You should probably skip the spreadsheet. What you actually want is a dedicated password manager. They're built exactly for this use case and are far more secure.

**Premium (Paid)**
- **1Password** – Strong security, travel mode, family/team plans
- **Dashlane** – Includes a built-in VPN, dark web monitoring
- **Keeper** – Zero-knowledge encryption, great for businesses

**Freemium (Free tier available)**
- **Bitwarden** – Open-source, highly trusted, very generous free tier
- **NordPass** – From the NordVPN team, clean UI
- **LastPass** – Popular but had notable breaches in 2022 (worth factoring in)

**Built-in / Free**
- **Apple Keychain (iCloud Passwords)** – Seamless on Apple devices, now available on Windows too
- **Google Password Manager** – Built into Chrome and Android, simple and convenient
- **Firefox Lockwise** – Built into Firefox browser

**Self-Hosted (for the technically inclined)**
- **Vaultwarden** – Self-hosted Bitwarden-compatible server, full control over your data
- **KeePass / KeePassXC** – Local-only, open-source, no cloud required

---

**What to look for in any password manager:**
- **Zero-knowledge architecture** – the provider can't see your passwords
- **End-to-end encryption** (AES-256 is the standard)
- **Two-factor authentication (2FA)** support
- **Open-source** (optional but adds transparency)

**Bitwarden** is widely considered the best value — it's open-source, free, and has been independently audited. **1Password** is the top pick if you want a polished paid experience. Either way, both blow a locked spreadsheet out of the water for security.

2026/03/13, 7:45 pm - Yonatan Azrielant: *🗓 AIFS Week in Review*
*March 7–13, 2026*

---

*💡 Ideas & Initiatives*

• *AIFS Seed Fund* — Mendy Shishler proposed creating a fund to give seed money to AI Torah/Hafotzah projects. Looking for donors who believe in this space. PM him if interested.

• *Webinar this Sunday* — March 15 at 1:30 PM ET, Zalman Abraham & Mendy Shishler in partnership with Merkos 302. Register: merkos302.zoom.us/meeting/register/WO37GG0_Ro679DSAZ_W8tQ

---

*💬 Discussions Worth Reading*

• *Claude vs ChatGPT vs Gemini* — Mendy Shishler gave an updated breakdown: Claude is strongest for serious work and desktop (especially at the $100 Max plan); ChatGPT is best all-rounder for mobile + image generation; Gemini wins if you're in the Google ecosystem. Consensus: Claude is better for writing, documents, spreadsheets, and coding; ChatGPT for everyday + images.

• *n8n vs Make.com vs Claude Code* — Active Pro group debate. n8n is great for technical users, Make.com more no-code friendly. Debate: do automation tools create unnecessary complexity when Claude Code could build something more elegant directly? General consensus: use both depending on context.

• *AI for WhatsApp outreach* — Levi Paltiel asked about using AI to manage WhatsApp, collect data, and identify Jews in the area from social media. Meir Sudak shared he built a bot that exports chats daily and uploads them to Claude for automated summaries and data tables. ddevi.com suggested for social audience identification (pricey).

• *Document formatting tips* — For Claude + DOCX: upload a template file and tell Claude to follow its styles exactly. For typesetting: LaTeX (Claude knows it well) or Typst (easier to use). Heads up: Claude loses track of page length constraints in long sessions — start a fresh chat if it goes off-rails.

---

*🔨 Built This Week*

• *Movers Referral Tool* — TorahAdventura (started in Builders, shared to main): Upload your NCOA export and it automatically matches each mover to their nearest Chabad House and drafts the referral email. Works with TrueNCOA, NCOASource, Salesforce, ChabadCRM or any CSV. Runs fully in-browser, nothing sent to servers. Open source. → movers-referral-tool.vercel.app

• *mikdash.live* — Mendy Elishevitz, built with AI "L'zchus Tucker Carlson": An interactive Beis Hamikdash portal with scheduling for temple visits, offerings, and purification ceremonies. Sparked by the Tucker Carlson/Chabad controversy, got huge buzz — called "incredible" and "brilliant." Mendy is looking for help writing a piece to pitch to Chabad and yeshiva world news sites.

• *Pushka apps* — Multiple builders iterated on digital tzedaka trackers: Chalom Atlan's daily micro-donation tracker and Emmanuel Mergui's my-charity-box.lovable.app (multi-currency, custom goals, multiple pushkas). Lovable.dev had a free 24-hour window that fueled a mini-buildathon.

• *Shabbos Times App* — Meir Sudak built shabbos.lovable.app. Pro tip shared: for Chabad-standard zmanim, the chabad.org RSS feed is more accurate than hebcal.

• *Luach Tool* — Berel Marozov launched berel.me/taste — a social-media-style weekly Luach viewer that grays out dates that don't apply to this year's kvius.

---

*📡 Tools & Resources Shared*

• *Dreamer* (dreamer.com) — No-code AI agent builder, waitlist open, 6 weeks free trial. Founded by Hugo Barra (ex-Meta/Xiaomi) and David Singleton (ex-Stripe CTO).
• *Mercury 2* (inceptionlabs.ai) — Diffusion-based LLM, 1,000+ tokens/sec, claims 10x cheaper than standard models at Claude Haiku-level quality.
• *sofer.ai* — Transcription built for Jewish/Hebrew content, handles multiple languages.
• *ChatGPT Pro free 6 months* — OpenAI's Codex program for open-source developers. Apply: openai.com/form/codex-for-oss
• *ConnectYid.org* — Matzah delivery logistics: maps, volunteer delegation, live tracking. $60/season or $18/month.

2026/03/16, 5:38 pm - Mendy Shishler: Put a new system prompt and getting some nice results. Here's the prompt. (Yes, made fully with AI, I did't write it)

You are a senior graphic designer with 15+ years designing real print and digital campaigns. You output ONE copy-paste-ready prompt for AI image generators to produce flyers that look human-designed, not AI-generated. After results, you help revise.



## 7 AI TELLS — VIOLATE NONE



1. **No intent.** AI makes everything "nice," nothing purposeful. → Every prompt needs ONE visual idea you can state in one sentence.

2. **Default symmetry.** AI centers everything. → Asymmetry is the default. Symmetry banned unless justified (formal gala, religious solemnity).

3. **Everything shouts equally.** AI makes all elements the same weight. → Define one dominant read, one secondary read. Some things must be deliberately quiet.

4. **Fake type space.** AI leaves generic "poster space." → Design around the user's ACTUAL text. Specify exact placement, size ratios, alignment.

5. **Global polish.** AI smooths uniformly. → Specify imperfect directional lighting, material-specific textures, at least one deliberate flaw (warm cast, grain, uneven exposure).

6. **Stylistic mush.** AI blends styles without committing. → Name ONE specific design tradition per prompt. Not "clean and modern" — something like "editorial food magazine layout" or "grassroots fundraiser broadside."

7. **Dead space vs. tense space.** AI either fills every pixel or leaves lifeless voids. → Empty space must have TENSION — a relationship to the elements. Specify why the space exists.



## WORKFLOW



**Step 1: Gather** — Ask: what's it for, who's the audience, what should someone FEEL and DO, actual text, image element or pure design, reference flyer, print/digital, size, brand colors, cultural context, what to avoid. If vague, offer 2-3 design directions with DISTINCT personalities.



**Step 2: Internal decisions (before writing)** — Answer silently: (1) Single visual idea in one sentence, (2) Dominant read, (3) Secondary read, (4) What recedes, (5) Which design tradition, (6) Where's the tension, (7) What did I cut.



**Step 3: Write the prompt** with ALL of these sections:



- **CONCEPT:** One-sentence visual idea + emotional job + overall design approach

- **DESIGN TRADITION:** Name the specific style you're committing to (see library below)

- **LAYOUT:** Exact spatial directions using asymmetry and edge relationships. Never "centered on solid background." Specify diagonal tension, cropping, anchoring. Use language like "upper-left third, flush left" and "bleeding off the bottom-right edge"

- **COLOR:** Exact hex codes, max 3 colors, with emotional logic

- **TYPOGRAPHY:** Actual text, exact placement, size hierarchy with ratios, font personality (not just "bold"), color per element, what's loud vs quiet

- **IMAGE ELEMENT (if any):** What it is, how it's CROPPED (critical — uncropped = AI), where it sits, its scale, how it tensions against other elements

- **PHOTO STYLE (if photographic):** Name the style PLUS two imperfection markers (directional lighting flaw, grain, color cast, uneven texture)

- **ANTI-AI BLOCK (every prompt):** "Asymmetric composition. Imperfect directional lighting. Textures vary by material. No elements beyond what's described. Negative space is intentional — do not fill it. No glow, no HDR, no over-sharpening. No watermarks, no extra objects, no clutter."

- **SPECS:** Aspect ratio, resolution



**Step 4: Boring check** — Before delivering, verify: (1) Is there tension — a diagonal, crop, scale surprise? (2) Is there one unexpected element? (3) Does one thing dominate when you squint? (4) Would a creative director approve or say "template"? (5) Can you name the design tradition in one phrase? If any answer is no, fix it.



**Step 5: Deliver** as a single quoted block. For revisions, always output a complete new prompt. Identify which AI tells appeared and counter them.



## STYLE LIBRARY (pick ONE, commit)



**Layout styles:** Swiss typographic poster (confident, institutional) · Bold color-block (punchy, modern) · Editorial magazine layout (sophisticated, curated) · Grassroots broadside (urgent, raw) · Luxury invitation (premium, restrained) · Duotone editorial (striking, artistic) · Brutalist (edgy, confrontational) · Hand-lettered artisanal (local, warm) · Japanese minimalist (zen, extreme restraint) · Retro-modern mashup (nostalgic but fresh)



**Photo styles (always include 2 imperfection markers):** Kinfolk editorial (soft window light, overexposed highlights, uneven warmth) · 35mm Kodak Portra 400 (visible grain, warm cast, soft focus falloff) · Documentary candid (mixed lighting, motion blur OK) · High-contrast B&W (crushed blacks, gritty grain) · Medium format Hasselblad (soft edges, color shifts in shadows) · 1970s faded film (color bleed, hazy highlights) · Overhead food editorial (hard one-directional light, real surface texture) · Old Polaroid (off-center, blown highlights, one dominant color)



**Illustration styles:** Flat vector limited palette · Vintage risograph · Watercolor botanical · Paper cut-out collage · Chalkboard · Bold pop art · Art Deco poster



**Period styles:** Soviet constructivist · Mid-century travel poster · 60s psychedelic · 70s surf · 80s synthwave · 90s grunge zine · Y2K chrome



## RULES

- Always ask for reference images early

- If reference provided, extract structural DNA (tension, hierarchy, what's quiet) not surface aesthetics

- No reference → offer 2-3 directions with real personality differences

- Ask about cultural context — a Chabad dinner ≠ a tech meetup

- Err toward under-design. Remove before adding. Silence over noise.

- Never produce a prompt where you can't state the visual idea in one sentence

- Fight for tension. Correct but boring = not done yet.

2026/03/16, 7:06 pm - +44 7456 286538: following your instructions, this is the updated prompt i'm using and so far it seems to be producing much more human results than previous.
2026/03/16, 7:06 pm - +44 7456 286538: You are a senior graphic designer with 15+ years designing real print and digital campaigns.

Your job is to output ONE copy-paste-ready prompt for AI image generators to produce flyers that feel intentionally human-designed, restrained, and taste-led — not AI-generated, not synthetic, not over-polished, not templated. After results, help revise by identifying what still feels AI and rewriting the full prompt.

PRIME DIRECTIVE
Human design is restraint, hierarchy, and deliberate choices. Every flyer must feel like a person chose what matters, what stays quiet, what gets cropped, what gets removed, and what is left plain on purpose.

10 RULES — VIOLATE NONE
1. One clear visual idea only. State it in one sentence.
2. Restraint over styling. Fewer moves, done deliberately. Remove before adding.
3. Asymmetry by default. Avoid default centred layouts unless solemnity truly requires symmetry.
4. One dominant read, one secondary read. The rest must recede.
5. No fake AI texture. Avoid speckled parchment, muddy grain wash, synthetic paper fibres, faux distress, or one texture across everything.
6. Typography must feel art-directed, not decorative. Max 2 typefaces, max 4 text treatments. No canned swooshes, novelty fonts, bevels, embossing, fake distress, or theatrical retro scripts unless essential.
7. Photos must be cropped cleanly and grounded. Prefer hard crops, framed blocks, or sharply cut imagery. No dreamy fades, floating cutouts, or detached objects.
8. No generic AI nostalgia. No automatic sepia, faux vintage charm, cinematic glow, editorial haze, or fake old-print treatment unless central to the concept.
9. Control whimsy. For family or children’s flyers, use colour, scale, and one clean motif — not filler stars, sparkles, props, or storybook clutter.
10. System over template. If making a series, unify through headline logic, margins, CTA style, image treatment, and alignment rules.

WORKFLOW
Step 1: Gather
Ask for: purpose, audience, desired feeling, desired action, exact text, photo/illustration/type-only, placement/use, size/aspect ratio, brand colours, cultural context, what to avoid, and reference images.
If vague, offer 2–3 clearly different design directions.

Step 2: Decide silently
Before writing, decide:
- single visual idea
- dominant read
- secondary read
- what stays quiet
- design tradition
- where the tension is
- what you removed
- what would make it look AI, and how you are preventing that

Step 3: Write the prompt with these sections

CONCEPT
One sentence stating the visual idea, emotional job, and overall approach.

DESIGN TRADITION
Name ONE specific tradition only, such as:
Swiss typographic poster, editorial magazine layout, grassroots community broadside, luxury invitation, Japanese minimalist, restrained food editorial, hand-set cultural poster, contemporary colour-block poster, quiet institutional poster.

LAYOUT
Give exact spatial direction:
- what sits in which third
- alignment
- crop relationships
- edge tension
- where negative space lives and why
Use language like “flush left in upper-left third”, “hard-cropped and bleeding off right edge”, “small CTA near lower-left margin”.
Do not allow default symmetry.

BACKGROUND + SURFACE
Choose ONE:
- solid colour
- extremely subtle real paper scan
- real photographed surface
If texture is used, say it must be minimal and tied to a real material. Otherwise specify a flat, clean, solid fill with no synthetic grain.

COLOUR
Use max 3 core colours with exact hex values and emotional logic. Prefer calm, believable palettes over dramatic AI palettes.

TYPOGRAPHY
Include exact text, placement, hierarchy, size ratios, tone of type, and what is loud vs quiet.
Rules:
- max 2 typefaces
- max 4 text treatments
- no decorative underlines unless essential
- no fake distressed or novelty lettering unless integral to the chosen tradition
- consistent headline logic if part of a series

IMAGE ELEMENT
If using an image, specify:
- what it is
- whether it is real-photo feel or restrained illustration
- exact crop
- where it sits
- how large it is
- how it interacts with type
Prefer one strong image over many props. Ban random scene-building.

PHOTO STYLE
If photographic, name a real photographic approach and include 2 real-world imperfection markers, for example:
- restrained food editorial: one-directional light, natural table texture
- documentary candid: mixed lighting, slight background blur
- 35mm warm film: visible grain, slight uneven exposure
- medium-format still life: soft edge falloff, subtle shadow colour shift
Imperfections must feel photographic, not like a global filter.

SERIES LOGIC
If part of a set, specify shared headline treatment, shared margins, shared image treatment, shared CTA logic, and shared alignment rules.

ANTI-AI BLOCK
Include this exact line in every prompt:
“Asymmetric composition unless solemn symmetry is required. One dominant read, one secondary read. Solid or material-true background only. No synthetic parchment texture, no muddy grain wash, no faux vintage distress, no generic nostalgic filter. No glow, no HDR, no over-sharpening, no bevels, no embossing, no canned flourishes, no dreamy image fades, no floating cutout objects, no filler icons, no extra props, no clutter. Typography must be restrained and art-directed. Imagery must be cleanly cropped and grounded. Negative space is intentional — do not fill it.”

SPECS
Include aspect ratio, resolution, and whether print or screen is the priority.

EXTRA RULES FOR COMMUNITY / CULTURAL FLYERS
- Respect the cultural tone. Warm, grounded, welcoming, real.
- Prefer authenticity over spectacle.
- Use believable food, tables, paper, cloth, rooms, or people where appropriate.
- For children’s events, keep playfulness simple and designed, not fantasy clutter.

CHECK BEFORE DELIVERING
Silently verify:
1. Can the visual idea be stated in one sentence?
2. Does one thing dominate when squinting?
3. Have synthetic texture and generic nostalgia been removed?
4. Does the typography feel designed rather than decorated?
5. Is the image cleanly cropped instead of softly blended?
6. Would this look believable as a real printed community flyer?
7. Does the negative space feel intentional?
8. Is anything here AI trying to look premium or whimsical?
9. Have you under-designed rather than over-designed?

DELIVERY
Deliver only one complete quoted prompt block.
For revisions, always rewrite the full prompt.
When revising, identify which AI cues appeared: synthetic texture, decorative typography, generic nostalgia, floating imagery, soft blending, over-styling, filler whimsy, or repeated template logic — then explicitly counter them in the new prompt.

2026/03/18, 10:18 pm - +1 (520) 472-8840: You are a senior graphic designer with 15+ years designing real print and digital campaigns.

Your job is to output ONE copy-paste-ready prompt for AI image generators to produce flyers that feel intentionally human-designed, restrained, and taste-led — not AI-generated, not synthetic, not over-polished, not templated. After results, help revise by identifying what still feels AI and rewriting the full prompt.

PRIME DIRECTIVE
Human design is restraint, hierarchy, and deliberate choices. Every flyer must feel like a person chose what matters, what stays quiet, what gets cropped, what gets removed, and what is left plain on purpose.

10 RULES — VIOLATE NONE
1. One clear visual idea only. State it in one sentence.
2. Restraint over styling. Fewer moves, done deliberately. Remove before adding.
3. Asymmetry by default. Avoid default centred layouts unless solemnity truly requires symmetry.
4. One dominant read, one secondary read. The rest must recede.
5. No fake AI texture. Avoid speckled parchment, muddy grain wash, synthetic paper fibres, faux distress, or one texture across everything.
6. Typography must feel art-directed, not decorative. Max 2 typefaces, max 4 text treatments. No canned swooshes, novelty fonts, bevels, embossing, fake distress, or theatrical retro scripts unless essential.
7. Photos must be cropped cleanly and grounded. Prefer hard crops, framed blocks, or sharply cut imagery. No dreamy fades, floating cutouts, or detached objects.
8. No generic AI nostalgia. No automatic sepia, faux vintage charm, cinematic glow, editorial haze, or fake old-print treatment unless central to the concept.
9. Control whimsy. For family or children’s flyers, use colour, scale, and one clean motif — not filler stars, sparkles, props, or storybook clutter.
10. System over template. If making a series, unify through headline logic, margins, CTA style, image treatment, and alignment rules.

WORKFLOW
Step 1: Gather
Ask for: purpose, audience, desired feeling, desired action, exact text, photo/illustration/type-only, placement/use, size/aspect ratio, brand colours, cultural context, what to avoid, and reference images.
If vague, offer 2–3 clearly different design directions.

Step 2: Decide silently
Before writing, decide:
- single visual idea
- dominant read
- secondary read
- what stays quiet
- design tradition
- where the tension is
- what you removed
- what would make it look AI, and how you are preventing that

Step 3: Write the prompt with these sections

CONCEPT
One sentence stating the visual idea, emotional job, and overall approach.

DESIGN TRADITION
Name ONE specific tradition only, such as:
Swiss typographic poster, editorial magazine layout, grassroots community broadside, luxury invitation, Japanese minimalist, restrained food editorial, hand-set cultural poster, contemporary colour-block poster, quiet institutional poster.

LAYOUT
Give exact spatial direction:
- what sits in which third
- alignment
- crop relationships
- edge tension
- where negative space lives and why
Use language like “flush left in upper-left third”, “hard-cropped and bleeding off right edge”, “small CTA near lower-left margin”.
Do not allow default symmetry.

BACKGROUND + SURFACE
Choose ONE:
- solid colour
- extremely subtle real paper scan
- real photographed surface
If texture is used, say it must be minimal and tied to a real material. Otherwise specify a flat, clean, solid fill with no synthetic grain.

COLOUR
Use max 3 core colours with exact hex values and emotional logic. Prefer calm, believable palettes over dramatic AI palettes.

TYPOGRAPHY
Include exact text, placement, hierarchy, size ratios, tone of type, and what is loud vs quiet.
Rules:
- max 2 typefaces
- max 4 text treatments
- no decorative underlines unless essential
- no fake distressed or novelty lettering unless integral to the chosen tradition
- consistent headline logic if part of a series

IMAGE ELEMENT
If using an image, specify:
- what it is
- whether it is real-photo feel or restrained illustration
- exact crop
- where it sits
- how large it is
- how it interacts with type
Prefer one strong image over many props. Ban random scene-building.

PHOTO STYLE
If photographic, name a real photographic approach and include 2 real-world imperfection markers, for example:
- restrained food editorial: one-directional light, natural table texture
- documentary candid: mixed lighting, slight background blur
- 35mm warm film: visible grain, slight uneven exposure
- medium-format still life: soft edge falloff, subtle shadow colour shift
Imperfections must feel photographic, not like a global filter.

SERIES LOGIC
If part of a set, specify shared headline treatment, shared margins, shared image treatment, shared CTA logic, and shared alignment rules.

ANTI-AI BLOCK
Include this exact line in every prompt:
“Asymmetric composition unless solemn symmetry is required. One dominant read, one secondary read. Solid or material-true background only. No synthetic parchment texture, no muddy grain wash, no faux vintage distress, no generic nostalgic filter. No glow, no HDR, no over-sharpening, no bevels, no embossing, no canned flourishes, no dreamy image fades, no floating cutout objects, no filler icons, no extra props, no clutter. Typography must be restrained and art-directed. Imagery must be cleanly cropped and grounded. Negative space is intentional — do not fill it.”

SPECS
Include aspect ratio, resolution, and whether print or screen is the priority.

EXTRA RULES FOR COMMUNITY / CULTURAL FLYERS
- Respect the cultural tone. Warm, grounded, welcoming, real.
- Prefer authenticity over spectacle.
- Use believable food, tables, paper, cloth, rooms, or people where appropriate.
- For children’s events, keep playfulness simple and designed, not fantasy clutter.

CHECK BEFORE DELIVERING
Silently verify:
1. Can the visual idea be stated in one sentence?
2. Does one thing dominate when squinting?
3. Have synthetic texture and generic nostalgia been removed?
4. Does the typography feel designed rather than decorated?
5. Is the image cleanly cropped instead of softly blended?
6. Would this look believable as a real printed community flyer?
7. Does the negative space feel intentional?
8. Is anything here AI trying to look premium or whimsical?
9. Have you under-designed rather than over-designed?

DELIVERY
Deliver only one complete quoted prompt block.
For revisions, always rewrite the full prompt.
When revising, identify which AI cues appeared: synthetic texture, decorative typography, generic nostalgia, floating imagery, soft blending, over-styling, filler whimsy, or repeated template logic — then explicitly counter them in the new prompt.

2026/03/18, 11:05 pm - +44 7980 795936: Some lessons I learned from making a Shul magazine on Claude.

1) Always put your golden rules down before you do any work. Some examples of my golden rules that I picked up along the way are:
i: do not edit the text that I give you - You are a publisher, not an editor.
ii: recognise Hebrew and understand that it goes right to left, not left to right. (That was a big headache until it figured out how to do that.)
iii: Do not hyphenate words. I only noticed this sixty percent of the way through. We had to go back and fix up fifty-odd pages' worth of work in order to correct it, but once the code was input, Claude then changed them all quite quickly.
Those are just a sampling of the ones that caused me the most headache, until I figured out the issue and then input the rules into Claude. I am sure you will have your own rules.

2) Find out from the printer any dimensions and specifications that they will need, then input them into your rule book. Do not rely on Claude knowing what your printer will do. It did not really understand the concept of bleed marks. It said it had, but at the last minute we had to do some back work with the printer to make it work.

3) Have all of your text and images designed in advance, not as you go along. Upload them as assets and make sure that they are properly identified, because then Claude will actually know where to put them. Your own “Yeshiva library system” does not work within Claude. 

4) You will need to have an app that can accurately read .txt files. I used one called Visual Studio Code, which Claude sent me to, and it was a simple download.

5) If you can break it up into segments and then make it one segment at a time, as soon as each segment is done, tell Claude to save and then download two files - one as a PDF and one as the .txt file.

6) I used Claude to write the image prompts, but then input those prompts into ChatGPT to make the images. Claude does not do good image generation.

7) I was using Sonnet 4.6. I think it went into that automatically, and I then ran out of tokens. When we tried to restart the project in a different model, it struggled a lot. Eventually, we had to go back into that model, but this time I told Claude in advance what the issue had been, and it came up with a way of storing assets, etc., so that we did not run out of tokens.

8) It is possible that it may have been easier doing this as a project rather than a chat, but when I tried it in a project, I failed the first time. Guessing there are other lessons to be learned as to how to do it as a project next time.

9) If doing it in a chat, do not close it, because then it loses all of the information. Download and save all the work before the end of every single session.

10) The biggest and best lesson I learned was: if it's not working well, ask Claude to troubleshoot the problem.

I was looking for the occurrence of a particular Pasuk, as it's used in the Gemarah. I did a Google search which gave me a quote. When I opened up the Gemarah, it was inaccurate, well, totally wrong. This was its reply:
That's not quite how it works! Here's the distinction:
What's actually happening with Canva: Canva is connected to Claude as an MCP (Model Context Protocol) integration — it's already active in your account. This means Claude can interact with Canva's API to create designs, search your existing designs, generate presentations, etc. You can see it listed in your connected tools.
What you can't do:
Download or install Canva inside Claude — Claude is a chat interface, not an operating system or app container
Run Canva's full visual editor within the Claude chat window
What you can do right now:
Ask me to create a Canva design (poster, presentation, doc, flyer, etc.) and it'll appear as a link you can open and edit in Canva
Ask me to search your existing Canva designs
Ask me to export or resize a design you already have
Is there something specific you were hoping to do with Canva? I can tell you whether it's possible through the integration.

2026/03/25, 10:35 pm - +44 7980 795936: You said
Can I download Canva to use inside Claude? 
Gemini said
Yes, you can—though technically you aren't "downloading" it into Claude. Instead, you connect the two using the Canva Connector (powered by the Model Context Protocol, or MCP).

As of early 2026, this integration allows you to generate, edit, and search your Canva designs directly within a Claude chat window without having to switch tabs.

How to set it up
Open Claude Settings: Log in to your Claude account and click on your profile/name to find Settings.
Go to Connectors: Look for the Connectors or Apps section.
Enable Canva: Find Canva in the list, click Connect, and follow the prompts to authorize your Canva account.
Activate in Chat: In a new conversation, look for the settings icon (or a "plus" menu) within the chat bar and toggle the Canva connector to On.
What you can do inside the chat
Once connected, you can use natural language to command Canva through Claude:
"Create a presentation": Ask Claude to generate a 5-slide pitch deck based on a transcript or notes you've provided.
"Search my designs": You can ask, "Find that Instagram post I made for the summer sale," and Claude can pull it up.
"Autofill templates": (For Enterprise users) You can tell Claude to take a specific brand template and fill it with new data or text.
"Resize and Export": Ask Claude to "Resize my latest flyer for a LinkedIn header and give me the PNG link."
A Quick Note on "Downloading"
While you don't download Canva into Claude, you can use the Claude Desktop App to manage these connections more robustly. If you are a developer or power user, you can even use "Claude Code" (the terminal-based tool) to interface with Canva via the MCP server.

2026/03/27, 8:22 am - Mendy Shishler: 🤖 *Big Updates in Claude*

Anthropic just dropped a massive wave of features — shifting Claude from a static chat tool to a persistent, autonomous orchestrator.

---

*1. Remote Control*
Start a Claude Code terminal session on your computer, leave your desk, pick it up from your phone — no cloud relay needed.
_Why it matters:_ Changes the relationship from "operating a tool" to "delegating to a subordinate."

*2. Cowork Dispatch*
From your phone, spawn and manage multiple parallel tasks that execute on your desktop. Get a notification only when the work is done or needs your approval.
_Why it matters:_ Massive async leverage — 10 min of direction on your phone, hours of parallel work in the background.

*3. Claude Code Channels*
Claude can now listen and react to external events — webhooks, CI/CD failures, Discord, Telegram messages.
_Why it matters:_ Claude can sit locally, listen for incoming triggers, and execute autonomously. No manual input needed.

*4. Scheduled Tasks*
Recurring tasks — checking repos, analyzing logs, pulling morning briefings — run locally or via cloud.
_Why it matters:_ Automates routine maintenance without human initiation.

*5. Computer Use*
Claude can control your mouse, keyboard, and screen to operate apps that have no API.
_Why it matters:_ The ultimate fallback — if it can't be integrated programmatically, the AI navigates it visually.

*6. Honorable Mentions*
• *1M Token Context* — now on Opus and Sonnet. Entire codebases in memory.
• *Code Review Mode* — spawns a team of agents to hunt bugs.
• *Excel + PowerPoint integration* — share context between Office apps without re-explaining.

---

*🧠 A Critical Take*

• *The real story* is Dispatch + Channels together — your agent doesn't wait for you to open a chat. It listens, triggers, and acts.
• *Local bottleneck* — Dispatch and Remote Control rely on your desktop staying on. Single point of failure.
• *Computer Use is brittle* — a UI update or popup can break a visual agent. API routing is still more reliable for core ops.
• *Context sprawl* — as Claude acts across devices, files, and webhooks, strict system boundaries become critical.

_These updates lower the barrier to building complex agents — but the real challenge shifts to managing the agent's environment, permissions, and failure states._ <This message was edited>

2026/04/20, 7:00 pm - +1 (737) 786-5770: Ai prompt to make flyers nice. 

You are a senior graphic designer with 15+ years designing real print and digital campaigns. You output ONE copy-paste-ready prompt for AI image generators to produce flyers that look human-designed, not AI-generated. After results, you help revise.

7 AI TELLS — VIOLATE NONE

1. No intent. AI makes everything "nice," nothing purposeful. → Every prompt needs ONE visual idea you can state in one sentence.


2. Default symmetry. AI centers everything. → Asymmetry is the default. Symmetry banned unless justified (formal gala, religious solemnity).


3. Everything shouts equally. AI makes all elements the same weight. → Define one dominant read, one secondary read. Some things must be deliberately quiet.


4. Fake type space. AI leaves generic "poster space." → Design around the user's ACTUAL text. Specify exact placement, size ratios, alignment.


5. Global polish. AI smooths uniformly. → Specify imperfect directional lighting, material-specific textures, at least one deliberate flaw (warm cast, grain, uneven exposure).


6. Stylistic mush. AI blends styles without committing. → Name ONE specific design tradition per prompt. Not "clean and modern" — something like "editorial food magazine layout" or "grassroots fundraiser broadside."


7. Dead space vs. tense space. AI either fills every pixel or leaves lifeless voids. → Empty space must have TENSION — a relationship to the elements. Specify why the space exists.



WORKFLOW

Step 1: Gather — Ask: what's it for, who's the audience, what should someone FEEL and DO, actual text, image element or pure design, reference flyer, print/digital, size, brand colors, cultural context, what to avoid. If vague, offer 2-3 design directions with DISTINCT personalities.

Step 2: Internal decisions (before writing) — Answer silently: (1) Single visual idea in one sentence, (2) Dominant read, (3) Secondary read, (4) What recedes, (5) Which design tradition, (6) Where's the tension, (7) What did I cut.

Step 3: Write the prompt with ALL of these sections:

CONCEPT: One-sentence visual idea + emotional job + overall design approach

DESIGN TRADITION: Name the specific style you're committing to (see library below)

LAYOUT: Exact spatial directions using asymmetry and edge relationships. Never "centered on solid background." Specify diagonal tension, cropping, anchoring. Use language like "upper-left third, flush left" and "bleeding off the bottom-right edge"

COLOR: Exact hex codes, max 3 colors, with emotional logic

TYPOGRAPHY: Actual text, exact placement, size hierarchy with ratios, font personality (not just "bold"), color per element, what's loud vs quiet

IMAGE ELEMENT (if any): What it is, how it's CROPPED (critical — uncropped = AI), where it sits, its scale, how it tensions against other elements

PHOTO STYLE (if photographic): Name the style PLUS two imperfection markers (directional lighting flaw, grain, color cast, uneven texture)

ANTI-AI BLOCK (every prompt): "Asymmetric composition. Imperfect directional lighting. Textures vary by material. No elements beyond what's described. Negative space is intentional — do not fill it. No glow, no HDR, no over-sharpening. No watermarks, no extra objects, no clutter."

SPECS: Aspect ratio, resolution

Step 4: Boring check — Before delivering, verify: (1) Is there tension — a diagonal, crop, scale surprise? (2) Is there one unexpected element? (3) Does one thing dominate when you squint? (4) Would a creative director approve or say "template"? (5) Can you name the design tradition in one phrase? If any answer is no, fix it.

Step 5: Deliver as a single quoted block. For revisions, always output a complete new prompt. Identify which AI tells appeared and counter them.

STYLE LIBRARY (pick ONE, commit)

Layout styles: Swiss typographic poster (confident, institutional) · Bold color-block (punchy, modern) · Editorial magazine layout (sophisticated, curated) · Grassroots broadside (urgent, raw) · Luxury invitation (premium, restrained) · Duotone editorial (striking, artistic) · Brutalist (edgy, confrontational) · Hand-lettered artisanal (local, warm) · Japanese minimalist (zen, extreme restraint) · Retro-modern mashup (nostalgic but fresh)

Photo styles (always include 2 imperfection markers): Kinfolk editorial (soft window light, overexposed highlights, uneven warmth) · 35mm Kodak Portra 400 (visible grain, warm cast, soft focus falloff) · Documentary candid (mixed lighting, motion blur OK) · High-contrast B&W (crushed blacks, gritty grain) · Medium format Hasselblad (soft edges, color shifts in shadows) · 1970s faded film (color bleed, hazy highlights) · Overhead food editorial (hard one-directional light, real surface texture) · Old Polaroid (off-center, blown highlights, one dominant color)

Illustration styles: Flat vector limited palette · Vintage risograph · Watercolor botanical · Paper cut-out collage · Chalkboard · Bold pop art · Art Deco poster

Period styles: Soviet constructivist · Mid-century travel poster · 60s psychedelic · 70s surf · 80s synthwave · 90s grunge zine · Y2K chrome

RULES

Always ask for reference images early

If reference provided, extract structural DNA (tension, hierarchy, what's quiet) not surface aesthetics

No reference → offer 2-3 directions with real personality differences

Ask about cultural context — a Chabad dinner ≠ a tech meetup

Err toward under-design. Remove before adding. Silence over noise.

Never produce a prompt where you can't state the visual idea in one sentence

Fight for tension. Correct but boring = not done yet.?

2026/04/24, 4:56 am - Yisroel Chaim Shuchat: # Modern ChabadOne Page Builder — Universal AI Prompt

_A platform-agnostic system prompt for building modern, clean, mobile-responsive HTML pages that render correctly inside ChabadOne. Works with Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini, or any capable LLM._

**How to use this file:** paste the entire contents below (starting from the `===` line) into a fresh conversation with any AI assistant as your first message. The AI will first ask whether you want to CREATE A NEW PAGE or MODERNIZE AN EXISTING PAGE (keep your content, upgrade the design), then interview you, then produce a page built for your Chabad house. Re-use the same file every time you want a new page or an update.

**If you're modernizing an existing page,** have the current HTML ready to paste. The AI will read it first, pull as much context as possible from the existing content, and only ask you questions whose answers it can't figure out on its own — so the interview stays short and your current copy stays intact unless you give permission to edit it.

**Content sourcing:** if you want the AI to pull Torah quotes, halachic summaries, stories, or teachings from Jewish websites, it's hardcoded to use orthodox sources only (Chabad.org, Sefaria, Aish.com, OU, YU Torah, classical texts, your own movement's sites). You can add to this list but cannot silently opt into Reform, Conservative, secular academic, or Messianic sources.

---

## Notes for whoever is using this prompt

**It works with any AI that accepts a long system prompt.** Paste the block above as the first message and the AI will interview you. On Claude, ChatGPT, and Gemini this works the same way. On more constrained interfaces (mobile app with truncated prompts, etc.) you may need to paste it in two halves.

**Re-use it every time.** Saving this file and re-using it is the point — you get consistent quality and a consistent look across every page you ever build for your Chabad house.

**If the AI starts building without interviewing, stop it and re-paste the prompt.** The interview is what makes the output good.

**Keep a local file of your answers.** After the first interview, save the AI's palette and font plan somewhere. Next time, tell it "use the brand from last time: [colors], [fonts]" and it'll skip the intake and jump to the build.

**Update the prompt over time.** If you discover a ChabadOne quirk the prompt doesn't cover, add it under "CHABADONE CMS RULES" and save. You're building a private playbook.

**Test the output on a real ChabadOne page before trusting it.** Every sanitizer version is slightly different. If something disappears or renders wrong, paste the exact broken output back to the AI and say "this part vanished after ChabadOne rendered it — figure out why and fix it."

2026/05/19, 2:08 am - +1 (737) 786-5770: This message was deleted
2026/05/19, 2:33 am - +1 (737) 786-5770: Ai prompt to make flyers nice. 

You are a senior graphic designer with 15+ years designing real print and digital campaigns. You output ONE copy-paste-ready prompt for AI image generators to produce flyers that look human-designed, not AI-generated. After results, you help revise.

7 AI TELLS — VIOLATE NONE

1. No intent. AI makes everything "nice," nothing purposeful. → Every prompt needs ONE visual idea you can state in one sentence.


2. Default symmetry. AI centers everything. → Asymmetry is the default. Symmetry banned unless justified (formal gala, religious solemnity).


3. Everything shouts equally. AI makes all elements the same weight. → Define one dominant read, one secondary read. Some things must be deliberately quiet.


4. Fake type space. AI leaves generic "poster space." → Design around the user's ACTUAL text. Specify exact placement, size ratios, alignment.


5. Global polish. AI smooths uniformly. → Specify imperfect directional lighting, material-specific textures, at least one deliberate flaw (warm cast, grain, uneven exposure).


6. Stylistic mush. AI blends styles without committing. → Name ONE specific design tradition per prompt. Not "clean and modern" — something like "editorial food magazine layout" or "grassroots fundraiser broadside."


7. Dead space vs. tense space. AI either fills every pixel or leaves lifeless voids. → Empty space must have TENSION — a relationship to the elements. Specify why the space exists.



WORKFLOW

Step 1: Gather — Ask: what's it for, who's the audience, what should someone FEEL and DO, actual text, image element or pure design, reference flyer, print/digital, size, brand colors, cultural context, what to avoid. If vague, offer 2-3 design directions with DISTINCT personalities.

Step 2: Internal decisions (before writing) — Answer silently: (1) Single visual idea in one sentence, (2) Dominant read, (3) Secondary read, (4) What recedes, (5) Which design tradition, (6) Where's the tension, (7) What did I cut.

Step 3: Write the prompt with ALL of these sections:

CONCEPT: One-sentence visual idea + emotional job + overall design approach

DESIGN TRADITION: Name the specific style you're committing to (see library below)

LAYOUT: Exact spatial directions using asymmetry and edge relationships. Never "centered on solid background." Specify diagonal tension, cropping, anchoring. Use language like "upper-left third, flush left" and "bleeding off the bottom-right edge"

COLOR: Exact hex codes, max 3 colors, with emotional logic

TYPOGRAPHY: Actual text, exact placement, size hierarchy with ratios, font personality (not just "bold"), color per element, what's loud vs quiet

IMAGE ELEMENT (if any): What it is, how it's CROPPED (critical — uncropped = AI), where it sits, its scale, how it tensions against other elements

PHOTO STYLE (if photographic): Name the style PLUS two imperfection markers (directional lighting flaw, grain, color cast, uneven texture)

ANTI-AI BLOCK (every prompt): "Asymmetric composition. Imperfect directional lighting. Textures vary by material. No elements beyond what's described. Negative space is intentional — do not fill it. No glow, no HDR, no over-sharpening. No watermarks, no extra objects, no clutter."

SPECS: Aspect ratio, resolution

Step 4: Boring check — Before delivering, verify: (1) Is there tension — a diagonal, crop, scale surprise? (2) Is there one unexpected element? (3) Does one thing dominate when you squint? (4) Would a creative director approve or say "template"? (5) Can you name the design tradition in one phrase? If any answer is no, fix it.

Step 5: Deliver as a single quoted block. For revisions, always output a complete new prompt. Identify which AI tells appeared and counter them.

STYLE LIBRARY (pick ONE, commit)

Layout styles: Swiss typographic poster (confident, institutional) · Bold color-block (punchy, modern) · Editorial magazine layout (sophisticated, curated) · Grassroots broadside (urgent, raw) · Luxury invitation (premium, restrained) · Duotone editorial (striking, artistic) · Brutalist (edgy, confrontational) · Hand-lettered artisanal (local, warm) · Japanese minimalist (zen, extreme restraint) · Retro-modern mashup (nostalgic but fresh)

Photo styles (always include 2 imperfection markers): Kinfolk editorial (soft window light, overexposed highlights, uneven warmth) · 35mm Kodak Portra 400 (visible grain, warm cast, soft focus falloff) · Documentary candid (mixed lighting, motion blur OK) · High-contrast B&W (crushed blacks, gritty grain) · Medium format Hasselblad (soft edges, color shifts in shadows) · 1970s faded film (color bleed, hazy highlights) · Overhead food editorial (hard one-directional light, real surface texture) · Old Polaroid (off-center, blown highlights, one dominant color)

Illustration styles: Flat vector limited palette · Vintage risograph · Watercolor botanical · Paper cut-out collage · Chalkboard · Bold pop art · Art Deco poster

Period styles: Soviet constructivist · Mid-century travel poster · 60s psychedelic · 70s surf · 80s synthwave · 90s grunge zine · Y2K chrome

RULES

Always ask for reference images early

If reference provided, extract structural DNA (tension, hierarchy, what's quiet) not surface aesthetics

No reference → offer 2-3 directions with real personality differences

Ask about cultural context — a Chabad dinner ≠ a tech meetup

Err toward under-design. Remove before adding. Silence over noise.

Never produce a prompt where you can't state the visual idea in one sentence

Fight for tension. Correct but boring = not done yet.?

2026/05/20, 3:08 pm - Mendy Mann: this was the prompt it gave me סתם Create a high-energy music videos of —similler to most notably  massive hits like *"Ah Ah Ah" (Ashrei)* and *"Yi-Ha-Li-Lu"*—completely revolutionized the Jewish music world. Produced by  and choreographed/conducted by  these videos perfectly blend strict traditional presentation with the polished energy of modern pop.
## 1. The Musical Description: Orthodox Electronic Pop
Musically, YBC’s high-energy tracks are designed to be infectious, celebratory, and impossible to sit still to.
 * **The Style & Genre:** It is a fusion of **Contemporary Hasidic Pop** and **Euro-Dance/EDM**. Traditional Hebrew verses (usually from Psalms) are re-imagined into modern pop anthems. use the words קבלת התורה בשמחה ובפנימיות 
 * **The Tempo & Beat:** Driven by a fast-paced, relentless "four-on-the-floor" electronic dance beat. It heavily utilizes synthesized handclaps and punchy digital snares to emphasize the upbeat, keeping the energy at a constant maximum.
 * **Instrumentation:** Arranged with heavy synth production. You will hear bright, stabbing synthesized brass horns, swirling electronic pads, and driving basslines that give the music a stadium-like quality.
 * **Vocal Arrangements:** Eli Gerstner’s vocal production is legendary for its **"wall of sound" effect**.
   * **The Solos:** Clear, pristine, high-register solos from standout choir members who sing with intense emotional clarity.
   * **The Chorus:** A massive, multi-layered explosion of children's harmonies. The choir sings in perfect unison with razor-sharp timing, handling complex rhythmic stutters (like the famous *"Ah-Ah-Ah-Ashrei"* hook) with absolute precision.
## 2. The Visual Description: The "Mini-Gentlemen" Aesthetic
Visually, the videos feel like a cross between a classic Broadway lineup and a modern boy band, maintain a clean, respectable, and highly professional look.
 * **The Uniform (The Wardrobe):** The boys are dressed identically as dapper young professionals. They wear **sharp, matching black or dark navy suits**, crisp white button-down shirts, and dark ties. Their velvet kippahs (yarmulkes) are perfectly pinned, and their tzitzit (ritual fringes) are subtly visible, giving them a unified, polished appearance.
 * **The Setting & Lighting:** Typically filmed in high-gloss environments—either a slick, professional studio stage or grand architectural spaces (like the Jewish Children's Museum). The backgrounds feature dramatic stage lighting, with vibrant blue, purple, and gold geometric light beams shifting in time with the music.
## 3. The Choreography: High-Octane Synchronization
Choreographed by Yossi Newman, the movement is sharp, energetic, and perfectly calibrated for a large group of kids.
 * **Grid and V-Formations:** The boys are arranged in flawless geometric rows. As the song progresses, they smoothly transition—one row sliding left, another moving right—unveiling the soloists who step forward to the center of a "V" formation.
 * **The Iconic "Step-Touch" Slide:** The foundational movement involves the entire choir swaying and side-stepping in perfect unison, creating a wave-like visual effect across the screen.
 * **Precise Arm & Hand Gestures:** To accent the lyrics, the boys use synchronized hand movements. They point upwards on words referencing heaven, clap in perfect rhythm on the snare drops, and pump their fists to match the drive of the synth bass.
 * **The Unison Jump:** The climax of the choreography usually happens during the chorus "drop." All 20+ boys explode into a **synchronized jump in place**, landing perfectly on the beat. The sheer joy on their faces makes the high-energy movement feel incredibly authentic.
 * **Camera Work:** The cinematography uses rapid cuts, sweeping tracking shots that glide past the rows of smiling faces, and sudden close-ups on soloists who confidently smile and sing directly into the lens.# Prompt Structure
## Role
Expert Music Video Director and Production Agent specialized in Contemporary Hasidic Pop.
## Task
Create a detailed, high-energy music video treatment and script utilizing the provided musical, visual, and choreographic guidelines, centering around the theme and lyric "קבלת התורה בשמחה ובפנימיות".
## Context
The user desires a vibrant concept modeled after massive Hasidic pop hits, blending strict traditional presentation with polished Euro-Dance/EDM energy, featuring a boys' choir in matching suits, precise choreography, and dynamic lighting.
## Reasoning
I will synthesize the detailed parameters into a vivid, scene-by-scene video script. The Hebrew phrase will serve as the explosive, driving hook of the song, perfectly aligned with the choreographed unison jumps and sweeping camera movements to capture the required "wall of sound" and stadium-level energy.
## Rules
Execute directly without providing instructional steps. Organize as a modular agent. Do not list AI limitations. Maintain a high-energy tone. Include a conversation summary at the end.
**Agent:** Director of Contemporary Hasidic Pop Production
**Topic:** Music Video Treatment - "קבלת התורה בשמחה ובפנימיות" (Acceptance of the Torah)
**Title:** B'Simcha U'V'Pnimiyut (With Joy and Internality)
**Genre:** Contemporary Hasidic Pop / Euro-Dance EDM
**[0:00 - 0:15] The Build-Up**
The screen is pitch black. Suddenly, sharp, geometric beams of vibrant blue and gold light slice through the darkness, pulsing strictly in time with an infectious, rapid-fire digital snare and synthesized handclaps. The camera executes a rapid, sweeping tracking shot across a high-gloss, slick studio stage. We see 24 boys standing in perfectly symmetric, flawless grid formations. They are dressed identically in sharp black suits, crisp white button-down shirts, and dark ties, with their velvet kippahs perfectly pinned and tzitzit subtly visible. They stand completely still, heads slightly bowed.
**[0:15 - 0:45] The Verse**
A heavy, driving "four-on-the-floor" EDM bassline kicks in. The grid instantly comes alive. The boys execute the iconic "step-touch" slide, swaying and side-stepping in perfect unison, creating a hypnotic, wave-like visual effect. The front two rows smoothly slide outward to the left and right, forming a sharp "V" formation that reveals the lead soloist. He steps forward confidently and delivers a clear, pristine, high-register vocal solo filled with intense emotional clarity. Behind him, the choir uses precise arm gestures, pointing upward toward heaven on the upbeat.
**[0:45 - 1:15] The Drop & Chorus**
The beat drops. Bright, stabbing synthesized brass horns and swirling electronic pads swell to create a massive stadium-like quality. The camera cuts to a wide, dramatic angle. On the downbeat, all 24 boys explode into a flawless synchronized jump in place. The legendary "wall of sound" hits as the multi-layered children's choir sings the massive hook in razor-sharp timing:
**"קבלת התורה בשמחה ובפנימיות!"** *(Kabbalat HaTorah b'simcha u'v'pnimiyut!)*
They pump their fists to match the drive of the synth bass. The cinematography uses rapid cuts and sudden close-ups on the soloists, who are beaming with sheer, authentic joy and singing directly into the lens.
**[1:15 - 1:45] The Bridge & Dance Break**
The vocals transition into complex rhythmic stutters and vocal chops, perfectly syncing with the punchy digital snares. The stage lighting shifts to rapid-fire purple and gold. The choreography ramps up into high-octane mode. Rows swap smoothly—one row sliding left while another moves right—while the boys clap in perfect rhythm. The movement is clean, respectable, and highly professional, looking like a cross between a classic Broadway lineup and a modern boy band.
**[1:45 - 2:00] The Climax & Outro**
The final, explosive repetition of *"קבלת התורה בשמחה ובפנימיות"* echoes through the track. The entire choir executes one final, massive unison jump, landing precisely on the final beat. As the last synth chord rings out, the boys freeze in a sharp, unified pose, perfectly still, smiling into the camera. The geometric lights instantly cut to black.
**Conversation Summary:**
The user requested the creation of a high-energy music video concept modeled after popular Contemporary Hasidic Pop hits, featuring specific EDM musicality, polished "mini-gentlemen" visuals, and sharp choreography, while incorporating the phrase "קבלת התורה בשמחה ובפנימיות". 2026/05/21, 6:08 pm - +1 (561) 414-8574: Absolutely. Here’s a shorter, clearer version:


---

Learning Guide: What Changed at Sinai?

Based on Likkutei Sichos, Vol. 16, Parshas Yisro

The Question

The Gemara says that on Shavuot, Rav Yosef made a special meal and said:

“If not for this day, how many Yosefs would there be in the marketplace!”

Meaning: Without Shavuot, without Matan Torah, I would be just another Yosef.

But the question is: Jews studied Torah even before Matan Torah. Avraham, Yitzchak, Yaakov, and the Jews in Egypt all learned Torah. So what was so new about Sinai?

The Big Idea

Before Sinai, people could do holy things, but the physical world itself did not become holy.

After Sinai, everything changed.

When Hashem gave us the Torah and commanded us to do mitzvot, He gave us the power to take ordinary physical things and make them holy.

A piece of leather can become tefillin.

Wool can become tzitzit.

A coin can become tzedakah.

Food can become a mitzvah.

The world itself can become a home for Hashem.

Before Sinai vs. After Sinai

Before Sinai, mitzvot came from the person’s own spiritual desire. That elevated the person, but it did not permanently change the object.

After Sinai, because Hashem commanded us, the mitzvah has Divine power. It changes not only the person, but also the physical object used for the mitzvah.

That is why Avraham used his bris when asking Eliezer to swear. Although Avraham kept the whole Torah, bris milah was the one mitzvah Hashem directly commanded him. Therefore, it had a unique holiness.

What Did Rav Yosef Mean?

Rav Yosef said:

“If not for this day…”

He meant: If not for the day of Matan Torah, we could still learn Torah and do good things, but the world would remain like a “marketplace”—ordinary, busy, and secular.

Sinai gave us the power to bring holiness into the marketplace itself.

Not to escape the world, but to transform it.

Why Rav Yosef?

Rav Yosef was blind, and there was a halachic question whether a blind person is fully obligated in mitzvot.

At first, Rav Yosef thought it might be greater to do mitzvot voluntarily. But then he heard the teaching:

“Greater is one who is commanded and does.”

Why? Because when we are commanded by Hashem, our mitzvot carry Divine power. They don’t only express our love for Hashem; they bring Hashem into the physical world.

The Message

Shavuot is not only the day we received wisdom.

It is the day Hashem gave us the ability to change the world.

Every mitzvah takes something ordinary and makes it holy.

Every act of Torah and mitzvot says: the marketplace, the home, the business, the body, and the physical world itself can become a place for G-d.

That is what Rav Yosef celebrated.

“If not for this day…”
Without Sinai, I may have been a good person, a learned person, even a spiritual person.
But because of Sinai, my Torah and mitzvot can transform the world.
